3.4     Counting Labeled Chordal Graphs with a Given

        Automorphism

   In this section, we describe the algorithm for Count Chordal Lab(n, π), which
counts the number of labeled chordal graphs with a given automorphism. This is the
algorithm of Theorem 6. In Section 3.5 we will prove correctness, analyze the running
time, and derive the corresponding sampling algorithm (Theorem 7).


Theorem 6 There is a deterministic algorithm that given n ∈ N and π ∈ Sn , computes
the number of labeled chordal graphs with vertex set [n] for which π is an automorphism.
This algorithm uses O(27µ n9 ) arithmetic operations, where µ = |Mπ |.


   In Chapter 2, we gave a dynamic-programming algorithm for computing the number
n-vertex labeled chordal graphs that uses O(n7 ) arithmetic operations, in the case when
we do not require the graphs to have a particular automorphism. Our algorithm in this
section is closely based on that one, but we add more arguments and more details to
each of the recurrences to keep track of the behavior of the automorphism. As was done
in Chapter 2, we evaluate the recurrences top-down using memoization.


3.4.1    Reducing from counting chordal graphs to counting con-

         nected chordal graphs

   For k ∈ N, let a(k) denote the number of labeled chordal graphs with vertex set
[k], and let c(k) denote the number of connected labeled chordal graphs with vertex set
[k]. Recall that the algorithm of Chapter 2 begins by reducing from counting chordal
graphs to counting connected chordal graphs via the following recurrence. (We omit the
“ω-colorable” requirement since we will not need that here.)

                                           118
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                        Chapter 3


Lemma 49 The number of labeled chordal graphs with vertex set [k] is given by
                               k        
                             X     k−1
                     a(k) =          ′−1
                                           c(k ′ )a(k − k ′ )
                             k′ =1
                                   k
for all k ∈ N.

   Here k ′ stands for the number of vertices in the connected component that contains
the label 1. The remaining connected components have a total of k − k ′ vertices. Since
this recurrence is relatively simple, most of the difficulty in the algorithm of Chapter 2
lies in the recurrences for counting connected chordal graphs. However, when counting
graphs with a given automorphism, the step of reducing to connected graphs is already
quite a bit more involved.
   Suppose we are given n ∈ N and π ∈ Sn as input. From now on, whenever we refer
to n or π, we mean these particular values from the input.
   We will first define a(k, p, M ) and c(k, p, M ), which are variations of a(k) and c(k)
that only count graphs for which π p |V (G) is an automorphism (see Definition 4). Here
π p stands for π to the power p, i.e., the permutation that arises from applying π a total
of p times. The reason for raising π to a power will be apparent in the recurrence for
a(k, p, M ). In the initial, highest-level recursive call, we will have p = 1 and thus π p = π.
   In Chapter 2, when counting the number of possibilities for a subgraph of size k ′
(for example, a connected component), the authors essentially relabel that subgraph to
have vertex set [k ′ ], so that one can count the number of possibilities using, for example,
c(k ′ ). In our algorithm, we want to relabel the vertices of each subgraph in a similar way.
However, this time, we do not relabel the vertices that are moved by π. This ensures
that the automorphism in each later recursive call will still be π, or a permutation closely
related to π.
   As a consequence, the vertex sets of the subgraphs that we wish to count become
slightly more complicated. For example, suppose we wish to count the number of possi-
                                             119
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                        Chapter 3


bilities for a 5-vertex subgraph that originally contains the moved vertices 2, 8, 9 ∈ Mπ .
Since we do not relabel the moved vertices, the resulting vertex set after relabeling is
{1, 2, 3, 8, 9} rather than {1, 2, 3, 4, 5}. More formally, suppose we have been given k ∈ N
and M ⊆ Mπ , where |M | ≤ k. Let V be the set of the first k − |M | natural numbers in
N \ Mπ . We define Vk,M := V ∪ M . For example, if k = 5 and M = Mπ = {2, 8, 9}, then
Vk,M = {1, 2, 3, 8, 9}. Intuitively, Vk,m is the label set of size k whose intersection with
Mπ is M and that otherwise contains labels that are as small as possible.
   For π̂ ∈ Sn and M ⊆ [n], recall that M is invariant under π̂ if π̂(i) ∈ M for all i ∈ M .
For M ′ , M ⊆ [n], we write M ′ ⊆π̂ M to indicate that M ′ ⊆ M and M ′ is invariant under
π̂. Intuitively, M ′ is a subset of M that respects the cycles of π̂ by taking all or nothing
of each cycle.


Definition 4 Suppose k, p ∈ [n] and suppose M ⊆πp Mπ , where |M | ≤ k. Let a(k, p, M )
(resp. c(k, p, M )) denote the number of labeled chordal graphs (resp. connected labeled
chordal graphs) with vertex set Vk,M for which π p |V (G) is an automorphism.


   Our algorithm returns a(n, 1, Mπ ). This is precisely the number of labeled chordal
graphs with vertex set [n] and automorphism π since Vn,Mπ = [n].
   Suppose we have been given fixed values of the arguments k, p, M of a(k, p, M ). Let
s be the smallest label in the vertex set Vk,M . For k ′ ∈ [k], let Pk′ be the family of sets
M ′ ⊆πp M such that |M |−k +k ′ ≤ |M ′ | ≤ k ′ and such that the following condition holds:
if s ∈ M , then s ∈ M ′ , and otherwise, |M ′ | ≤ k ′ − 1. This last condition will ensure that
s belongs to the connected component of size k ′ in the recurrence for a(k, p, M ).
   Suppose C ⊆ [n], σ ∈ Sn . For an element i ∈ C, we say the period of i with respect to
(C, σ) is the smallest positive integer j such that σ j (i) ∈ C. Let Q be the family of sets
C ⊆ M such that all elements of C have the same period j ≥ 2 with respect to (C, π p ),
and such that s ∈ C. For a set C ∈ Q, we write pC to denote the period of the elements
                                             120
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                            Chapter 3


of C (with respect to (C, π p )), and we let Cσ := C ∪ σ(C) ∪ · · · ∪ σ pC −1 (C) denote the
union of the sets that C is mapped to by powers of σ, where σ = π p .
    To compute a(k, p, M ), we use the following recurrence. The dot in front of the curly
braces denotes multiplication. Note that we have |M ′ | ≤ k ′ and |M \ M ′ | ≤ k − k ′ by
the definition of Pk′ .


Lemma 50 Let k, p ∈ [n] and suppose M ⊆πp Mπ , where |M | ≤ k. We have
                                                                    
                                                                     k−|M |
                                                                             

                                                                                    if s ∈ M
   a(k, p, M ) =         c(k ′ , p, M ′ )a(k − k ′ , p, M \ M ′ ) ·

                                                                                 

                   ′                                                   ′

    For some intuition, suppose G is a graph counted by a(k, p, M ), and let C be the
connected component of G that contains s. The first line of the recurrence for a(k, p, M )
covers the case when C is invariant under π p . This case is analogous to Lemma 49. In
the first summation, k ′ stands for |C| and M ′ stands for the set of vertices in C that can
be moved by π p . If s ∈ M , then in addition to the vertices in M ′ , we need to choose
k ′ − |M ′ | additional vertices for C so that |C| = k ′ . For these, we must choose non-moved
vertices, so there are k − |M | possible vertices to choose from. If s ∈
                                                                       / M , then we subtract
1 from each of the numbers in the binomial coefficient since we already know that s is a
non-moved vertex in C.
    The second line covers the case when C is not invariant under π p , which means all
of C is mapped to some other connected component of G by π p . In this case, we have
pC components of size |C| that are all isomorphic to C, and the rest of the graph has
k − pC |C| vertices. We do not need a binomial coefficient in this case because all of the
vertices in C are moved. There are c(|C|, p · pC , C) possibilities for the edges of C since
π p·pC is an automorphism of C. As an example, suppose p = 1. If we apply π p = π to the
                                                   121
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                       Chapter 3


vertices of C a total of pC times, then the image π pC (C) is equal to C, although these
two sets might not match up pointwise. To ensure that the image π pC (C) matches up
with the edges of C, we require that π pC is an automorphism of C. This is why we need
the argument p in a(k, p, M ) and c(k, p, M ).
   Note that for a graph G counted by a(k, p, M ), we have Mπp ∩V (G) ⊆ Mπ ∩V (G) ⊆ M
since V (G) = Vk,M . This means no vertex in V (G) \ M is moved by π p , so we are free to
relabel these vertices in the proof of Lemma 50 without changing the automorphism. In
the initial recursive call a(n, 1, Mπ ), we have p = 1 and M = Mπ , so Mπp ∩ V (G) = M .
Later on in the algorithm, it is possible that some vertices in M are not actually moved
by π p . For example, in the previous paragraph, it could happen that π p·pC is the identity
on C (and then p · pC becomes the new value of p). However, we always have M ⊆ Mπ
by the definition of a(k, p, M ), so if |Mπ | = µ, then |M | ≤ µ. This fact will be useful for
the running time analysis in Section 3.5.3.
   The proof of Lemma 50, along with the proofs of all of the following recurrences, can
be found in Section 3.5.


3.4.2     Recurrences for counting connected chordal graphs

   To compute c(k, p, M ), we will define various counter functions that are analogous to
those in Definition 3 from Chapter 2. The counter functions for our algorithm will be
similar, except we only want to count graphs with a particular automorphism. Therefore,
we will have several more arguments in addition to the usual ones t, x, ℓ, k, z from Def-
inition 3. As above, the argument p will indicate that π p is the current automorphism.
We also introduce the arguments MX , ML , MZ , and MK , each of which is a subset of
Mπ . Roughly, these sets specify which vertices — in X, L := LG (X), Z := [z], and the
rest of the graph, respectively — can be moved by the current permutation (but the sets


                                             122
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                        Chapter 3


X, L, and Z will be modified slightly).
   In Definition 3, the g-functions count graphs with vertex set [x + k], and the f -
functions count graphs with vertex set [x + ℓ + k]. For our algorithm, we will make
some adjustments to these vertex sets to ensure that the vertices moved by the current
permutation still appear in the graph. This is similar to how we defined Vk,M above. To
do so, we define several symbols for these vertex sets and vertex subsets (Vargs , etc.).
Each of these depends on the list of arguments of the function in question, which we
                                                                                  
                                                                  t x       k    z
denote by args. For example, when defining g, we have args =
                                                                  p MX MK MZ
(see Definition 5).
   First, suppose args comes from one of the g-functions in Definition 5. Let VX be
the set of the first x − |MX | natural numbers in N \ Mπ , and let VK be the set of the
first k − |MK | natural numbers in N \ (VX ∪ Mπ ). We define Xargs := VX ∪ MX and
Vargs := Xargs ∪ VK ∪ MK . Also, if z is included in args, then let VZ be the set of the
first z − |MZ | natural numbers in N \ Mπ . In this case, we define Zargs := VZ ∪ MZ .
   For the other case, suppose args comes from one of the f -functions. Let VX be the
set of the first x − |MX | natural numbers in N \ Mπ , let VL be the set of the first ℓ − |ML |
natural numbers in N \ (VX ∪ Mπ ), and let VK be the set of the first k − |MK | natural
numbers in N \ (VX ∪ VL ∪ Mπ ). We define Xargs := VX ∪ MX , Largs = VL ∪ ML , and
Vargs := Xargs ∪Largs ∪VK ∪MK . Also, if z is included in args, then let VZ be the set of the
first z − |MZ | natural numbers in N \ Mπ . In this case, we again define Zargs := VZ ∪ MZ .
   These vertex subsets still have the same sizes as they did in the original algorithm:
the size of Vargs is x + k or x + ℓ + k, |Xargs | = x, |Largs | = ℓ, the rest of the graph has
size k, and |Zargs | = z. Just as we had Z ⊆ X in the original algorithm, we now have
Zargs ⊆ Xargs . This is because we require MZ ⊆ MX in Definition 5, and we also require
z − |MZ | ≤ x − |MX |, which implies VZ ⊆ VX .
   For g-functions, we have Mπp ∩ Vargs ⊆ MX ∪ MK , and for f -functions, we have
                                             123
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                                    Chapter 3


Mπp ∩ Vargs ⊆ MX ∪ ML ∪ MK , by the definition of Vargs . We are now ready to define
our new counter functions.

3.5       Proofs of Theorems 6 and 7

    In this section, we prove correctness of the recurrences from Section 3.4 and complete
the proofs of Theorems 6 and 7.


3.5.1      Automorphism properties

    First of all, we show that automorphisms preserve evaporation time.


Lemma 61 Suppose G is a labeled chordal graph that evaporates at time t with exception
set X, where X ⊆ V (G) is a clique, and let L1 , . . . , Lt be the evaporation sequence. If π̂
is an automorphism of G such that X is invariant under π̂, then Li is invariant under π̂
for all i ∈ [t].


     Proof: We proceed by induction on i. Let L

   For the induction step, suppose L1 , . . . , Li are all invariant under π̂ for some i ∈ [t−1].

   Next, we prove two lemmas that relate the automorphisms of a graph G to the
automorphisms of its induced subgraphs.


Lemma 62 Let G be a labeled chordal graph with an automorphism π̂ and induced sub-
graphs G1 and G2 such that V (G1 ) ∪ V (G2 ) = V (G). If X := V (G1 ) ∩ V (G2 ) and
A := V (G1 ) \ V (G2 ) are both invariant under π̂, then π̂|V (G1 ) is an automorphism of G1
and π̂|V (G2 ) is an automorphism of G2 .


    Proof: We know π̂|V (G1 ) is a bijection from V (G1 ) to V (G1 ) since X and A are
invariant under π̂. Moreover, this map is in fact an automorphism of G1 since π̂ is an
automorphism of G and G1 is an induced subgraph. Similarly, π̂|V (G2 ) is a bijection from
V (G2 ) to V (G2 ) since X and V (G2 ) \ V (G1 ) are invariant under π̂, and this map is in
fact an automorphism G2 .


Lemma 63 Let G be a labeled chordal graph with induced subgraphs G1 and G2 such
that V (G1 ) ∪ V (G2 ) = V (G) and such that there are no edges from V (G1 ) \ V (G2 ) to
V (G2 ) \ V (G1 ). If π̂ is a permutation of V (G) such that π̂|V (G1 ) is an automorphism of
G1 and π̂|V (G2 ) is an automorphism of G2 , then π̂ is an automorphism of G.


    Proof: Let u, v ∈ V (G). If u and v both belong to V (G1 ), then u and v are adjacent
in G1 if and only if π̂(u) and π̂(v) are adjacent in G1 since π̂|V (G1 ) is an automorphism of
G1 . Since G1 is an induced subgraph of G, this means u and v are adjacent in G if and
only if π̂(u) and π̂(v) are adjacent in G. The same statement also holds if u, v ∈ V (G2 ).
                                              132
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                         Chapter 3


The only remaining case is when one vertex is in V (G1 ) \ V (G2 ) and the other is in
V (G2 ) \ V (G1 ). In this case, u and v are not adjacent in G, and neither are π̂(u) and
π̂(v) since V (G1 ) ∩ V (G2 ) is invariant under π̂.


3.5.2     Proofs of recurrences

   Recall that we are given n and π as input. The proofs of all of these recurrences build
upon the proofs of the analogous recurrences in Chapter 2. For this reason, we highly
recommend reading the proof of correctness of the counting algorithm in Chapter 2 before
reading this section in detail. In the proof of almost every recurrence here, we will cite
the analogous lemma from Chapter 2 to prove various properties such as the evaporation
time of each subgraph.

Remark 2 One important piece of the original proofs in Chapter 2 is that for each
recurrence, the authors describe two injective functions. For example, in the proof of the
recurrence for g(t, x, k, z), there is an injective function that maps each graph G counted
by g(t, x, k, z) to a tuple (G1 , G2 , A), as well as an injective function that maps each tuple
(G1 , G2 , A) to such a graph G. In this chapter, we only prove injectivity in detail in the
proof of the recurrence for g̃, since this statement for each of the other recurrences is
either similar to g̃ or simpler than g̃. This can be found in Lemmas 64 and 65, after the
recurrence for g̃.

   We use uppercase letters to denote the set of graphs counted by each of the counter
functions. For example, the set of graphs counted by a(k, p, M ) is denoted by A(k, p, M ),
                                                                                     
                                 t x     k     z                       t x      k     z
the set of graphs counted by g                     is denoted by G                         ,
                                p MX MK MZ                             p MX MK MZ
and so on. We also use similar notation for the original counter functions: G(t, x, k, z)
is the set of graphs counted by g(t, x, k, z), G(t,
                                               e x, k, z) is the set of graphs counted by

g̃(t, x, k, z), and so on. We proceed in the order in which our counter functions were
                                              133
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                            Chapter 3


defined, so that similar functions and proofs appear consecutively. (This is somewhat
different from the ordering of the lemma numbers.)

applying ϕ(D \ M, Vk−k′ ,M \M ′ \ M ) to the labels in D \ M . We have Mπp ∩ Vk,M ⊆ M by
the definition of Vk,M , so by only relabeling vertices outside of M , we know that we have
not relabeled any vertices that are moved by π p . Thus π p |V (G1 ) and π p |V (G2 ) continue
to be automorphisms of G1 and G2 . Clearly D \ M = D \ (M \ M ′ ). We also have
Vk−k′ ,M \M ′ \ M = Vk−k′ ,M \M ′ \ (M \ M ′ ) since Vk−k′ ,M \M ′ \ (M \ M ′ ) is disjoint from

has the vertex set Vk−k′ ,M \M ′ . Since G1 is connected, we have G1 ∈ C(k ′ , p, M ′ ) and
G2 ∈ A(k − k ′ , p, M \ M ′ ).

component of G by π p . Let C1 = C, and let Ci = π p (Ci−1 ) for 2 ≤ i ≤ pC . Let Gi = G[Ci ]
for i ∈ [pC ]. The graphs G2 , . . . , GpC are all isomorphic to G1 since π p , . . . , π p(pC −1) are
automorphisms of G. (This ensures that the map that takes G to (G1 , G′ , C) is injective,
where G′ is as defined below.)
    For the remainder of the graph, let D = V (G) \ (C1 ∪ · · · ∪ CpC ), and let G′ = G[D].

where k̂ = k − pC |C| and M̂ = M \ Cπp . We know π p·pC |V (G1 ) is an automorphism of G1
since π p·pC |V (G) is an automorphism of G that maps C to itself. We also know π p |V (G′ )
is an automorphism of G′ , so G1 ∈ C(|C|, p · pC , C) and G′ ∈ A(k − pC |C|, p, M \ Cπp ).
Hence a(k, p, M ) is at most the summation above.

     / M . Let C = M ′ ∪ Ĉ, and let D = Vk,M \ C. We construct a graph G as follows:
if s ∈

Relabel G1 by applying ϕ(Vk′ ,M ′ \ M ′ , Ĉ) to the labels in Vk′ ,M ′ \ M ′ , and relabel G2 by
applying ϕ(Vk−k′ ,M \M ′ \ M, D \ M ) to the labels in Vk−k′ ,M \M ′ \ M . Now let G be the
union of G1 and G2 (by taking the union of the vertex sets and the edge sets). Since
π p |V (G1 ) is an automorphism of G1 and π p |V (G2 ) is an automorphism of G2 , this means
π p |V (G) is an automorphism of G, so we have G ∈ A(k, p, M ).
   For the second case, suppose we are given a set C ∈ Q, a graph G1 ∈ C(|C|, p · pC , C),
and a graph G′ ∈ A(k − pC |C|, p, M \ Cπp ). Let G2 , . . . , GpC all be equal to G1 initially.

G′ . The label sets of G1 , . . . , GpC are all pairwise disjoint since C ∈ Q. If we apply π p to
all of the labels in Gi for some 1 ≤ i < pC , then the resulting graph is Gi+1 . Furthermore,
if we apply π p to the labels in GpC , then the resulting graph is the same as the graph we
obtain if we apply π p·pC to the labels in G1 . Since π p·pC |V (G1 ) is an automorphism of G1 ,
this graph is in fact equal to G1 . This means π p |V (Ĝ) is an automorphism of Ĝ, where Ĝ
is the union of G1 , . . . , GpC . Since we also know π p |V (G′ ) is an automorphism of G′ , this
implies that π p |V (G) is an automorphism of G. Therefore, G ∈ A(k, p, M ), so a(k, p, M )
is at least the summation above.

Lemma 64 Let ψ1 be the map from Ginv to the set of tuples (G1 , G2 , k ′ , x′ , M, M ′ , Ĉ, X̂ ′ )
with certain properties from the first case of the “≤” direction of the proof of Lemma 55,
and let ψ2 be the map from Gmove to the set of tuples (G1 , G′ , x′ , C, M ′ , X̂ ′ ) with certain
properties from the second case. The maps ψ1 and ψ2 are injective.


        Proof: To see that ψ1 is injective, suppose G and G⋆ are distinct graphs in Ginv .
We want to show ψ1 (G) ̸= ψ1 (G⋆ ). Suppose that starting from G, we obtain ψ1 (G) =
(G1 , G2 , k ′ , x′ , M, M ′ , Ĉ, X̂ ′ ), along with the sets C, D, and X ′ as described in Lemma 55.
Suppose that starting from G⋆ , we obtain ψ1 (G⋆ ) = (G⋆1 , G⋆2 , k ′⋆ , x′⋆ , M ⋆ , M ′⋆ , Ĉ ⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ),
   8
       See Footnote 3 in Lemma 54.

                                                   143
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                                                Chapter 3


along with the sets C ⋆ , D⋆ , and X ′⋆ . If (k ′ , x′ , M, M ′ , Ĉ, X̂ ′ ) ̸= (k ′⋆ , x′⋆ , M ⋆ , M ′⋆ , Ĉ ⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ),
then we are done, so suppose (k ′ , x′ , M, M ′ , Ĉ, X̂ ′ ) = (k ′⋆ , x′⋆ , M ⋆ , M ′⋆ , Ĉ ⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ). This
means C = C ⋆ since C = M ∪ Ĉ and C ⋆ = M ⋆ ∪ Ĉ ⋆ , which implies D = D⋆ . Similarly,
we also have X ′ = X ′⋆ .
    Since G ̸= G⋆ , we either have G[X ′ ∪C] ̸= G⋆ [X ′ ∪C] or G[Xargs ∪D] ̸= G⋆ [Xargs ∪D].
If G[X ′ ∪ C] ̸= G⋆ [X ′ ∪ C], then after relabeling, G[X ′ ∪ C] still differs from G⋆ [X ′ ∪ C]
since we use the same sets M = M ⋆ and M ′ = M ′⋆ in both of these relabeling procedures.
Thus in this case, we have G1 ̸= G⋆1 . Otherwise, if G[Xargs ∪ D] ̸= G⋆ [Xargs ∪ D], then
after relabeling, G[Xargs ∪ D] still differs from G⋆ [Xargs ∪ D] since we use the same sets
MX and MK when relabeling, so we have G2 ̸= G⋆2 . Therefore, ψ1 is injective.
    To see that ψ2 is injective, suppose G and G⋆ are distinct graphs in Gmove . We
want to show ψ2 (G) ̸= ψ2 (G⋆ ). Suppose that starting from G, we obtain ψ2 (G) =
(G1 , G′ , x′ , C, M ′ , X̂ ′ ), along with the sets C1 , . . . , CpC , D, X1′ , . . . , Xp′ C and the graphs
G2 , . . . , GpC as described in Lemma 55, where C1 = C. Also, suppose that starting
from G⋆ , we obtain ψ2 (G⋆ ) = (G⋆1 , G′⋆ , x′⋆ , C ⋆ , M ′⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ), along with the sets C1⋆ , . . . , Cp⋆C ,
D⋆ , X1′⋆ , . . . , Xp′⋆C , and the graphs G⋆2 , . . . , G⋆pC , where C1⋆ = C ⋆ . If (x′ , C, M ′ , X̂ ′ ) ̸=
(x′⋆ , C ⋆ , M ′⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ), then we are done, so suppose (x′ , C, M ′ , X̂ ′ ) = (x′⋆ , C ⋆ , M ′⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ). We
have Ci = Ci⋆ for all i ∈ [pC ] since C = C ⋆ , using the fact that Ci = π p (Ci−1 ) and
Ci⋆ = π p (Ci−1
            ⋆
                ). This implies D = D⋆ . Similarly, we also have Xi′ = Xi′⋆ for all i ∈ [pC ]
since X1′ = X1′⋆ .
    Since G ̸= G⋆ , we either have G[Xi′ ∪ Ci ] ̸= G⋆ [Xi′ ∪ Ci ] for some i ∈ [pC ] or G[Xargs ∪
D] ̸= G⋆ [Xargs ∪ D]. First, suppose we have G[Xi′ ∪ Ci ] ̸= G⋆ [Xi′ ∪ Ci ] for some i ∈ [pC ].
For all 2 ≤ i ≤ pC , π p(i−1) is an isomorphism from G1 to Gi , since π p , . . . , π p(pC −1) are
automorphisms of G. Similarly, for all 2 ≤ i ≤ pC , π p(i−1) is an isomorphism from G⋆1
to G⋆i . Therefore, we have G[X1′ ∪ C1 ] ̸= G⋆ [X1′ ∪ C1 ]. After relabeling, G[X1′ ∪ C1 ]
still differs from G⋆ [X1′ ∪ C1 ] since we use the same set M ′ = M ′⋆ in both of these
                                                          144
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                                                       Chapter 3


relabeling procedures. Thus in this case, we have G1 ̸= G⋆1 . Otherwise, if G[Xargs ∪ D] ̸=
G⋆ [Xargs ∪ D], then after relabeling, G[Xargs ∪ D] still differs from G⋆ [Xargs ∪ D] since
we use the same sets MX and MK when relabeling, so we have G2 ̸= G⋆2 . Therefore, ψ2
is injective.


Lemma 65 Let ψ1′ be the map from the set of tuples (G1 , G2 , k ′ , x′ , M, M ′ , Ĉ, X̂ ′ ) with
certain properties to Ginv from the first case of the “≥” direction of the proof of Lemma 55,
and let ψ2′ be the map from the set of tuples (G1 , G′ , x′ , C, M ′ , X̂ ′ ) with certain properties
to Gmove from the second case. The maps ψ1′ and ψ2′ are injective.


     Proof: To see that ψ1′ is injective, suppose

              (G1 , G2 , k ′ , x′ , M, M ′ , Ĉ, X̂ ′ ) and (G⋆1 , G⋆2 , k ′⋆ , x′⋆ , M ⋆ , M ′⋆ , Ĉ ⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ )

are distinct tuples in the domain of ψ1′ that map to G and G⋆ , respectively. If k ′ ̸= k ′⋆ ,
then either M ̸= M ⋆ or Ĉ ̸= Ĉ ⋆ since k ′ = |M | + |Ĉ| and k ′⋆ = |M ⋆ | + |Ĉ ⋆ |. Similarly,
if x′ ̸= x′⋆ , then X̂ ′ ̸= X̂ ′⋆ or M ′ ̸= M ′⋆ . Therefore, we know (G1 , G2 , M, M ′ , Ĉ, X̂ ′ ) ̸=
(G⋆1 , G⋆2 , M ⋆ , M ′⋆ , Ĉ ⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ). Let X ′ = M ′ ∪ X̂ ′ and X ′⋆ = M ′⋆ ∪ X̂ ′⋆ .
    First, suppose (M, Ĉ) ̸= (M ⋆ , Ĉ ⋆ ), which means M ∪ Ĉ ̸= M ⋆ ∪ Ĉ ⋆ . After we relabel
G1 , the vertex set of G1 \ X ′ is M ∪ Ĉ, which contains s. Similarly, after we relabel G⋆1 ,
the vertex set of G⋆1 \ X ′⋆ is M ⋆ ∪ Ĉ ⋆ , which also contains s. Therefore, the component
of G \ Xargs that contains s has a different label set from the component of G⋆ \ Xargs
that contains s, so G ̸= G⋆ .
    Next, suppose (M ′ , X̂ ′ ) ̸= (M ′⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ), which means X ′ ̸= X ′⋆ . The neighborhood of
the component of G\Xargs that contains s is X ′ , and the neighborhood of the component
of G⋆ \ Xargs that contains s is X ′⋆ . Hence G ̸= G⋆ .
    Lastly, suppose (G1 , G2 ) ̸= (G⋆1 , G⋆2 ) and (M, M ′ , Ĉ, X̂ ′ ) = (M ⋆ , M ′⋆ , Ĉ ⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ), since
this is the only remaining possibility. If G1 ̸= G⋆1 , then either the component of G \ Xargs
                                                            145
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                                           Chapter 3


that contains s will differ from the component of G⋆ \ Xargs that contains s, or the
edges from those components to Xargs will differ. Similarly, if G2 ̸= G⋆2 , then either the
remaining components of G \ Xargs will differ from those of G⋆ \ Xargs , or the edges from
those components to Xargs will differ. Hence G ̸= G⋆ , so ψ1′ is injective.
    To see that ψ2′ is injective, let (G1 , G′ , x′ , C, M ′ , X̂ ′ ) and (G⋆1 , G′⋆ , x′⋆ , C ⋆ , M ′⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ) be
distinct tuples in the domain of ψ2′ that map to G and G⋆ , respectively. By a similar argu-
ment to the first paragraph above, we know (G1 , G′ , C, M ′ , X̂ ′ ) ̸= (G⋆1 , G′⋆ , C ⋆ , M ′⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ).
As before, let X ′ = M ′ ∪ X̂ ′ and X ′⋆ = M ′⋆ ∪ X̂ ′⋆ .
    If C ̸= C ⋆ , then a similar argument to the second paragraph shows that G ̸= G⋆ .
If (M ′ , X̂ ′ ) ̸= (M ′⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ), then a similar argument to the third paragraph shows that
G ̸= G⋆ . Lastly, if (G1 , G′ ) ̸= (G⋆1 , G′⋆ ) and (C, M ′ , X̂ ′ ) ̸= (C ⋆ , M ′⋆ , X̂ ′⋆ ), then a similar
argument to the fourth paragraph shows that G ̸= G⋆ . Hence ψ2′ is injective.

Lemma 55, except for the two sums over x′ : In both summations, rather than summing
over x′ such that 1 ≤ x′ ≤ x, we sum over x′ such that 1 ≤ x′ ≤ x − 1.


     Proof: The argument for g̃p is similar to the proof for g̃, except we do not allow
x′ = x since no component of G \ Xargs sees all of Xargs .

3.5.3        Running time

       The running time is dominated by the arithmetic operations needed to compute f˜p .
In the first part of the recurrence for f˜p (corresponding to when C is invariant under
π p ), there are three summations that sum over at most n possible values (the sums over
k ′ , x′ , and ℓ′ ), and there are three summations that sum over at most 2µ possible values
(the sums over M , MX′ , and ML′ ). As for the arguments, there are six arguments whose
values are at most n (t, x, ℓ, k, z, p), and there are four arguments that are sets of moved
points with at most 2µ possible values (MX , ML , MK ). The analysis for the second part
of the recurrence (when C is not invariant) is similar. Therefore, the overall running time
is O(27µ n9 ).
  23
       See Footnote 3 in Lemma 54.



                                               157
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                                 Chapter 3


3.5.4      Sampling labeled chordal graphs with a given automor-

           phism

    The algorithm for Sample Chordal Lab(n, π) is given by the following theorem.


Theorem 7 There is a randomized algorithm that given n ∈ N and π ∈ Sn , generates a
graph uniformly at random from the set of all labeled chordal graphs with vertex set [n]
for which π is an automorphism. This algorithm uses O(27µ n9 ) arithmetic operations,
where µ = |Mπ |.


    In Chapter 2, the authors used the standard sampling-to-counting reduction of [1] to
derive an algorithm for sampling labeled chordal graphs from the corresponding counting
algorithm. For labeled chordal graphs with a given automorphism, Theorem 7 follows
from Theorem 6 in exactly the same way.



3.6       Bound on the Number of Labeled Chordal Graphs

          with Automorphism π

    In this section, we prove a bound on the number of n-vertex labeled chordal graphs
that have a given automorphism π, in terms of n and the number of moved points of π
(Theorem 8). To do this, we begin by bounding the number of graphs of this form that
have a given perfect elimination ordering (PEO) and a given maximum clique size. In
fact, we only need to consider PEOs that delete some maximum clique of G as late as
possible. The following observation shows that every chordal graph has such a PEO.


Observation 7 For every n-vertex chordal graph G with maximum clique size ω, there
exists a PEO v1 , . . . , vn of G such that G \ {v1 , . . . , vn−ω } is a clique of size ω.

                                                 158
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                         Chapter 3


     Proof: Let C be a maximum clique in G. For each i ∈ [n − ω], by Lemma 40 we can
always find a vertex vi that is simplicial in G \ {v1 , . . . , vi−1 } and does not belong to C.
After that, the remaining ω vertices of the graph can be added to the PEO in any order,
since all of these are simplicial in G \ {v1 , . . . , vn−ω }.
    Suppose G is an n-vertex chordal graph with automorphism π and maximum clique
size ω. Imagine that we delete the vertices of G in order according to a given PEO that
has the property from Observation 7. For our purposes, it makes sense to truncate this
PEO once the graph that remains becomes a clique of size ω because at that point, there
is only one possibility for the edges of the remaining graph. A truncated PEO of an
n-vertex chordal graph G with maximum clique size ω is an ordering v1 , . . . , vn−ω of a
subset S ⊆ V (G) such that V (G) \ S is a clique of size ω and such that v1 , . . . , vn−ω can
be extended to a perfect elimination ordering v1 , . . . , vn of G. By Observation 7, every
chordal graph has a truncated PEO.
    For most of the lemmas in this section, we will count graphs that have the truncated
PEO 1, 2, . . . , n − ω. For n ∈ N, 1 ≤ ω ≤ n, and π ∈ Sn , let απ (n, ω) denote the number
of labeled chordal graphs G with vertex set [n] and maximum clique size exactly ω such
that π is an automorphism of G and such that 1, 2, . . . , n − ω is a truncated PEO of G.
Let Aπ (n, ω) denote the set of such graphs.
    We only need to bound the number of graphs with this particular truncated PEO
because the number of graphs is the same regardless of which truncated PEO we choose.
More formally, suppose v1 , v2 , . . . , vn−ω is an ordering of a subset of [n] of size n − ω.
For n, ω, and π as above, let βπ (n, ω) denote the number of labeled chordal graphs G
with vertex set [n] and maximum clique size exactly ω such that π is an automorphism
of G and such that v1 , v2 , . . . , vn−ω is a truncated PEO of G. Note that απ (n, ω) and
βπ (n, ω) have different truncated PEOs. The following observation shows that this makes
no difference.
                                                 159
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                             Chapter 3


Observation 8 Let n ∈ N, 1 ≤ ω ≤ n, π ∈ Sn . We have απ (n, ω) = βπ (n, ω).


       Proof: Let v1 , . . . , vn be an ordering of [n] that begins with v1 , . . . , vn−ω , and let
ψ : [n] → [n] be the bijection defined by ψ(i) = vi for all i ∈ [n]. We have the following
bijection between απ (n, ω) and βπ (n, ω): given a graph G ∈ απ (n, ω), relabel the vertices
of G by applying ψ to the label of each vertex.
      Our goal is now to prove a bound on απ (n, ω). It will be useful to consider two
different cases having to do with which points are moved by π.


      Two cases for the moved points. Suppose we are given n ∈ N, 1 ≤ ω ≤ n, and
π ∈ Sn . Let Ĉ = {n − ω + 1, n − ω + 2, . . . , n}. This is the clique of size ω that would
remain after deleting the truncated PEO 1, 2, . . . , n − ω from a graph G ∈ Aπ (n, ω). Let
µ = |Mπ |. The permutation π falls into one of the following cases:

  1. |Mπ ∩ Ĉ| ≤ 9µ
                 10
                                    9
                    , i.e., at most 10 of the moved points of π belong to Ĉ

  2. |Mπ ∩ Ĉ| > 9µ
                 10
                    .

We prove bounds for these two cases in Lemmas 71 and 72. However, Lemma 71 assumes
ω ≥ 9n
    20
       and µ ≤ 4n
                5
                  , and Lemma 72 assumes ω ≤ n − µ5 . We begin by dealing with the
corresponding edge cases (when ω is small or close to n, or µ is close to n) in Lemmas 67
to 70 since these cases are relatively simple and use ideas that will also be useful later
on.


Lemma 66 Let n ∈ N, 1 ≤ ω ≤ n. The number of labeled chordal graphs G with vertex
set [n] and maximum clique size exactly ω such that 1, 2, . . . , n − ω is a truncated PEO
of G is at most
                                             nn 2ω(n−ω) .


                                                 160
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                          Chapter 3


     Proof: For 0 ≤ i ≤ n − ω, let Ai denote the set of labeled chordal graphs G
with vertex set {n − ω − i + 1, . . . , n} and maximum clique size exactly ω such that
n − ω − i + 1, . . . , n − ω is a truncated PEO of G. We prove |Ai | ≤ ni 2ωi by induction on
i, for all i ∈ {0, 1, . . . , n − ω}. When i = 0, there is just one graph in A0 , namely a clique
of size ω. Now let i ∈ [n − ω] and suppose the statement is true for i − 1. If we delete the
vertex with label n − ω − i + 1 from a graph G ∈ Ai , then the resulting graph (say G′ )
belongs to Ai−1 . We claim that there are at most n2ω possibilities for the neighborhood
of this vertex in G. The neighborhood must be a clique since this vertex is simplicial in
G, and thus it is contained in some maximal clique of G′ . By Lemma 41, G′ has at most
n maximal cliques. To choose this neighborhood, we can first choose a maximal clique
of G′ (which has size at most ω) and then choose a subset of it. Therefore, there are at
most n2ω possibilities. Since G′ ∈ Ai−1 , the number of possible graphs in Ai is at most
n2ω |Ai−1 | ≤ n2ω ni−1 2ω(i−1) ≤ ni 2ωi . This gives us the desired bound when i = n − ω.
   We can use Lemma 66 to bound the number of chordal graphs with small or large
values of ω.

of G is at most
                                             2         2

     Proof: This follows immediately from Lemma 68 since µ ≤ n, and thus ω > 4n
                                                                              5
                                                                                .
    Next, we will deal with the case when µ > 4n
                                               5
                                                 (Lemma 70). When a graph Ĝ has an
automorphism π, it is necessary that every subgraph G of Ĝ “respects” π, in the sense
that the edges of G obey whichever properties are required by π. More precisely, this
requirement is defined as follows. In Definition 6, one can think of G as being a subgraph
of a graph with vertex set [n] for which π is an automorphism.


Definition 6 Let n ∈ N, π ∈ Sn , and let G be a labeled graph such that V (G) ⊆ [n]. We
say G respects π if whenever a pair of vertices a, b ∈ V (G) and a number 0 ≤ p ≤ n − 1
satisfy π p (a), π p (b) ∈ V (G), we have the following: a is adjacent to b if and only if π p (a)
is adjacent to π p (b).


    For a graph G with vertex set [n], G respects π if and only if π is an automorphism
of G. Imagine that we build up a graph G ∈ Aπ (n, ω) by starting with an empty vertex
                                                  162
Sampling Unlabeled Chordal Graphs in Expected Polynomial Time                        Chapter 3


set and then adding each of the vertices n, n − 1, . . . , 1 to the graph, in that order. It is
clear that the current graph must respect π at every step of this process.
   Next, the following definition will be useful for bounding the number of possible
neighborhoods of a vertex.

Definition 7 Let n ∈ N, 1 ≤ ω ≤ n, π ∈ Sn . Let 1 ≤ a ≤ n − ω, let V ′ = {a + 1, . . . , n},
and let b ∈ [n] be the largest number in the cycle of π that contains a. For c ∈ N, we
say the element a is 1c -retrospective with respect to ω and π if a ̸= b and the following
holds: for every M ⊆ V ′ such that ω − ωc ≤ |M | ≤ ω, we have
                                                           ω
                                     |π p (M ) ∩ V ′ | ≥     ,
                                                           c
where p is the number such that π p (a) = b.

   To see the intuition behind Definition 7, again imagine that we build up a graph
G ∈ Aπ (n, ω) by adding the vertices n, n−1, . . . , 1 to the graph, in that order. Each time
we add a vertex a to the current graph (which has vertex set V ′ ), we want to bound the
                                                                      1
number of possible neighborhoods for this vertex. Suppose vertex a is 20 -retrospective.
We know vertex b has already been added to the graph since b > a. The neighborhood
of vertex a is contained in some maximal clique M of G[V ′ ]. If |M | is much smaller than
ω, then there clearly are few possibilities for this neighborhood. On the other hand, if
                                 ω                            1
|M | is close to ω (at least ω − 20 ), then the definition of 20 -retrospective says that a
significant portion of the edges/non-edges from a to vertices in M can be copied from
edges/non-edges incident to b that have already been decided in the past since π is an
automorphism. In particular, the relationship between b and every vertex of π p (M ) ∩ V ′
has already been decided. Therefore, there are strictly fewer than n2ω possibilities for
the neighborhood of vertex a.
   Using this notion, we can prove a bound that is tighter than Lemma 66 for the case
when µ > 4n
          5
            . By the above lemmas, we can assume ω is neither too large nor too small.

                                                   168
Chapter 4

Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex
Deletion Problems in FPT Time


4.1      Introduction

   For a graph class G, Vertex Deletion to G is the decision problem where the input
is a graph G and a nonnegative integer k and the goal is to decide whether there exists a
vertex subset S ⊆ V (G) of size at most k such that G \ S ∈ G. In this chapter, we work
with three particular classes of undirected graphs. Let GI be the class of edgeless graphs
(i.e., the independent set graphs), let GC be the class of cluster graphs, and let GS be the
class of split graphs. When the graph class in question is GI , GC , or GS , the problem of
Vertex Deletion to G is known as Vertex Cover, Cluster Vertex Deletion,
or Split Vertex Deletion, respectively. In this chapter, we present algorithms to
solve the corresponding graph counting problems. In particular, for each of the above
graph classes G, we design an FPT algorithm to compute the number of n-vertex graphs
that are yes-instances of Vertex Deletion to G. In other words, given n and k as
input, we compute the number of n-vertex graphs G such that G \ S ∈ G for some

                                            169
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                        Chapter 4


S ⊆ V (G) of size at most k.
   We also consider one class of directed graphs. A tournament is a directed graph G such
that for every pair of vertices u, v ∈ V (G), exactly one of (u, v) or (v, u) is a directed edge
of G. Let GA be the class of acyclic tournaments. The problem of Vertex Deletion
to GA is the decision problem where the input is a tournament G and a nonnegative
integer k and the goal is to decide whether there exists a vertex subset S ⊆ V (G) of size
at most k such that G \ S ∈ GA . This problem is also known as Feedback Vertex
Set in Tournaments. In this chapter, we design an FPT algorithm to compute the
number of n-vertex tournaments that are yes-instances of Vertex Deletion to GA .
   To achieve this, we design a general framework that reduces each of these four counting
problems to a simpler version of the problem with given deletion sets. For each of the
problems in question, we then design an FPT algorithm for the version with given deletion
sets. Overall, the algorithms for counting yes-instances of Vertex Cover, Cluster
Vertex Deletion, and Feedback Vertex Set in Tournaments have running
                 2k+o(k)
time at most 23            · nO(1) , and the running time of the algorithm for counting yes-
instances of Split Vertex Deletion is a triple-exponential function of k. We also
design a faster algorithm for counting yes-instances of Vertex Cover that runs in time
    2
k O(k ) n3 , using ideas inspired by the Buss kernel for that problem.



4.2      Preliminaries

   In this chapter, we implicitly assume all graphs that we consider are labeled graphs.
A directed graph G is acyclic if and only if there is a total order ≤ on V (G) such that
u ≤ v for every directed edge (u, v) [36]. Such an ordering is called a topological ordering.
Furthermore, it is easy to see that every acyclic tournament has a unique topological
ordering. In an undirected graph G, distinct vertices u and v are called true twins if

                                              170
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                       Chapter 4


N (u) = N (v) and (u, v) ∈ E(G). The vertices u and v are false twins if N (u) = N (v)
and (u, v) ∈
           / E(G). An ordered partition is a partition that comes with a total order on
the set of its parts. For a set A, we write 2A to denote the power set of A.



4.3      Framework for Several FPT Graph Counting Al-

         gorithms

    Suppose G is one of our three classes of undirected graphs (GI , GC , or GS ). We are
given n and k as input, and our goal is to compute the number of n-vertex graphs G such
that G \ S ∈ G for some S ⊆ V (G) of size at most k. The main idea of our algorithm is to
repeatedly use the inclusion-exclusion principle to reduce this problem to the following:
given sets S1 , . . . , Sp and n as input, count the number of n-vertex graphs G such that
G \ Si ∈ G for all 1 ≤ i ≤ p. This reduction is essentially the same no matter which
graph class we are dealing with. For the problem that we reduce to, however, each of
the above graph classes needs its own algorithm that is tailored to that particular graph
class.
    The algorithm for counting yes-instances of Feedback Vertex Set in Tourna-
ments follows a similar approach with directed graphs, but for now, we first describe
the algorithm for a class G of undirected graphs.


4.3.1     Reducing to the problem with given sets

    To begin with, we describe a general framework for counting yes-instances of Vertex
Deletion to G, assuming we have an algorithm for the above problem in which the
sets S1 , . . . , Sp are given in the input. The identifier γ will specify which graph class we
are dealing with. For example, Gγ = GI if γ = I.

                                             171
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                                Chapter 4


Definition 8 Let γ ∈ {I, C, S}. For a graph G, we say S ⊆ V (G) is a Gγ -deletion set
of G if G \ S ∈ Gγ . We say a Gγ -deletion set S of G is minimal if no proper subset of
S is a Gγ -deletion set of G.


       For every graph class Gγ that we consider, there is a well-known bound on the number
of minimal Gγ -deletion sets of a given graph. Every graph has at most 2k minimal vertex
covers of size at most k since the bounded search tree in the standard branching algorithm
for Vertex Cover has at most 2k leaves. For Cluster Vertex Deletion, the
standard bounded search tree for this problem has at most 3k leaves since a graph is
a cluster graph if and only if it has no induced P3 . Hence every graph has at most 3k
minimal GC -deletion sets of size at most k. For the class of split graphs, a graph is split
if and only if it does not contain a cycle on four or five vertices or a 2K2 (two disjoint
edges) as as induced subgraph. Thus the same bounded search tree argument shows that
every graph has at most 5k minimal GS -deletion sets of size at most k.1
       We define the function gγ (k) to be this bound for each graph class Gγ . In other words,
gI (k) = 2k , gC (k) = 3k , and gS (k) = 5k for all k ≥ 0.


Theorem 9 Let γ ∈ {I, C, S}, and let g(k) = gγ (k). Let n ∈ N, 0 ≤ k ≤ n, and let
S1 , . . . , Sp be subsets of [n] of size at most k, where 1 ≤ p ≤ (k + 1)g(k). Suppose we have
an algorithm that, given n and the sets S1 , . . . , Sp as input, computes the number of graphs
G with vertex set [n] such that G \ Si ∈ Gγ for all i ∈ [p]. Suppose the running time of
this algorithm is f (k)q(n). Then there is an algorithm that given n ∈ N and 0 ≤ k ≤ n,
computes the number of graphs G with vertex set [n] such that G \ S ∈ Gγ for some
S ⊆ V (G) of size at most k. The running time of this algorithm is g(k)O(k·g(k)) f (k)q(n).
                                                                                2
   1
    The FPT algorithm in [82] implies that there are in fact at most 2k+O(log k) minimal GS -deletion
sets of size at most k. However, this is not explicitly stated in that paper, so for completeness, we use
the bound of 5k in our results.

Definition 9 For a tournament G, we say S ⊆ V (G) is a GA -deletion set of G if
G \ S ∈ GA . We say a GA -deletion set S of G is minimal if no proper subset of S is a
Gγ -deletion set of G.

   Every tournament has at most 3k minimal GA -deletion sets of size at most k, since a
tournament contains a directed cycle if and only if it contains a triangle (i.e., a directed
cycle of length 3). Let g(k) be this bound, i.e., g(k) = 3k for all k ≥ 0.

Theorem 10 Let n ∈ N, 0 ≤ k ≤ n, and let S1 , . . . , Sp be subsets of [n] of size at most
k, where 1 ≤ p ≤ (k + 1)g(k). Suppose we have an algorithm that, given n and the
sets S1 , . . . , Sp as input, computes the number of tournaments G with vertex set [n] such
that G \ Si ∈ GA for all i ∈ [p]. Suppose the running time of this algorithm is f (k)q(n).
Then there is an algorithm that given n ∈ N and 0 ≤ k ≤ n, computes the number of
tournaments G with vertex set [n] such that G \ S ∈ GA for some S ⊆ V (G) of size at
most k. The running time of this algorithm is g(k)O(k·g(k)) f (k)q(n).

   The algorithm for Theorem 10 is exactly the same as the algorithm for Theorem 9,
except every time we had a “graph,” we now have a “tournament,” and every time we
had a “Gγ -deletion set,” we now have a “GA -deletion set.” All that remains is to analyze
the running time.


4.3.2     Running time

   At the start of the algorithm for Theorem 9, we first compute all of the necessary
binomial coefficients that appear in the sum from Equation (4.1). We can compute all
                                            176
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                        Chapter 4

            a
            
values of   b
                such that 0 ≤ a ≤ n and 0 ≤ b ≤ a in O(n2 ) time.
   The algorithm begins with Equation (4.1), where we iterate over all J ∈ C. This
amounts to iterating over all collections {S1 , . . . , Sp } of subsets of [n] of size at most k
such that 1 ≤ p ≤ g(k) and pi=1 Si = [ℓ], where ℓ = | pi=1 Si |. The largest possible
                              S                                  S

              S                       S

Equation (4.1), and if not, we discard this collection of sets. The reason we require the

4.3.3     The algorithm for GI

   To complete the algorithm for the graph class GI , we need to design an algorithm for
the problem in which the sets S1 , . . . , Sp are given in the input. The following lemma
gives us a convenient way of characterizing graphs that have a given collection of vertex
covers.


Lemma 75 Let n ∈ N, and let S1 , . . . , Sp be subsets of [n], where p ≥ 1. Let A = pi=1 Si ,
                                                                                   S

B = [n] \ A, and T = pi=1 Si . Suppose G is a graph with vertex set [n]. Then Si is a
                    T

vertex cover of G for all i ∈ [p] if and only if G has the following properties:

  1. Si is a vertex cover of G[A] for all i ∈ [p]

  2. B is an independent set

  3. G has no edges with one endpoint in A \ T and the other in B.


    Proof: First, suppose Si is a vertex cover of G for all i ∈ [p]. Property (1) is clearly
true, and we know B is an independent set since S1 is a vertex cover of G. If u ∈ A \ T
and v ∈ B, then u and v are not adjacent since there is some set Si that does not contain
u. Thus property (3) holds as well.
   For the other direction, suppose properties (1)-(3) hold for G. Let i ∈ [p]. Every edge
in G[A] is incident to some vertex in Si by property (1). The only edges of G that do
not belong to G[A] are the edges from T to B, and all of these are incident to a vertex
in Si since T ⊆ Si . Hence Si is a vertex cover of G.
   To count how many graphs have all of the given vertex covers, we simply need to count
how many graphs satisfy properties (1)-(3). Let A, B, T be as defined in Lemma 75.


Lemma 76 Let n ∈ N, 0 ≤ k ≤ n, and let S1 , . . . , Sp be subsets of [n] of size at most k,
where 1 ≤ p ≤ (k + 1)2k . There is an algorithm that computes the number of graphs G
                                            178
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                      Chapter 4


with vertex set [n] such that Si is a vertex cover of G for all i ∈ [p]. The running time
                         4 2k
of this algorithm is 2O(k 2 ) · n2 log2 n.

     Proof: Algorithm. Let A be the set of graphs GA with vertex set A such that Si
is a vertex cover of GA for all i ∈ [p]. To compute |A|, we iterate over all graphs with
vertex set A, and for each such graph, we check whether every set Si is a vertex cover of
the graph. Let t = |T | and b = |B|. Return 2tb |A|.

   Correctness. This algorithm computes the number of graphs with vertex set [n]
that satisfy properties (1)-(3) from Lemma 75. The set A is the set of possibilities for
G[A], and for each fixed choice of G[A], there are 2tb possible ways of choosing the edges
from T to B. Thus the number of graphs that satisfy properties (1)-(3) is 2tb |A|.

   Running time. We have |A| ≤ (k 2 + k)2k , so the number of graphs with vertex set
                         4 2k                  2     k
A is at most 2h(k) ≤ 2O(k 2 ) , where h(k) = (k +k)2
                                                 2
                                                       . It is easy to check whether each
Si is a vertex cover of each graph with vertex set A in O(n2 ) time. Computing t and b
takes O(np) time, where p ≤ (k + 1)2k . Using repeated squaring, we can compute 2tb
in O(log n) multiplications, each of which takes n2 log n time since each number has at
                                                                          4 2k
most O(n2 ) bits [91]. Hence the running time of this algorithm is 2O(k 2 ) · n2 log2 n.
   By Theorem 9, this gives us an algorithm that computes the number of n-vertex
graphs with a vertex cover of size at most k. The overall running time is g(k)O(k·g(k)) f (k)·
                                               4 2k
n2 log2 n, where g(k) = 2k and f (k) = 2O(k 2 ) . This simplifies to a running time of
    4 2k
2O(k 2 ) · n2 log2 n.


4.3.4      The algorithm for GC

   Next, we follow similar steps to design an algorithm for GC , the class of cluster graphs.
Suppose G is a graph with vertex set V (G) ⊆ [n] for some n ∈ N, and suppose we have
been given a collection of subsets S1 , . . . , Sp of [n]. We say an edge (u, v) ∈ E(G) is
                                             179
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                      Chapter 4


perennial if {u, v} ∩ Si = ∅ for some i ∈ [p]. The intuition is that a perennial edge is
sometimes alive (not deleted) when we consider the graphs G \ S1 , . . . , G \ Sp . A non-edge
of G is a pair of distinct vertices u, v ∈ V (G) such that (u, v) ∈
                                                                  / E(G). We similarly say
a non-edge (u, v) of G is perennial if {u, v} ∩ Si = ∅ for some i ∈ [p].


Lemma 77 Let n ∈ N, and let S1 , . . . , Sp be subsets of [n], where p ≥ 1. Let A = pi=1 Si ,
                                                                                   S

B = [n] \ A, and T = pi=1 Si . Suppose G is a graph with vertex set [n]. Then G \ Si is
                    T

a cluster graph for all i ∈ [p] if and only if G has the following properties:

  1. G[A] \ Si is a cluster graph for all i ∈ [p]

  2. G[B] is a cluster graph

  3. For every v ∈ A \ T , N (v) ∩ B is either the empty set or is a connected component
      of G[B]

  4. For every u, v ∈ A \ T , if (u, v) is a perennial edge, then N (u) ∩ B = N (v) ∩ B

  5. For every u, v ∈ A \ T , if (u, v) is a perennial non-edge, then N (u) ∩ B ̸= N (v) ∩ B
      unless N (u) ∩ B = N (v) ∩ B = ∅.


    Proof: First, suppose G \ Si is a cluster graph for all i ∈ [p]. Property (1) is clearly
true, and we know G[B] is a cluster graph since G \ S1 is a cluster graph. For property
(3), let v ∈ A \ T . The vertex v belongs to some cluster graph G \ Sj since there is
some set Sj that does not contain v. If v were to have a neighbor w1 in one connected
component of G[B], along with a neighbor w2 in a distinct connected component of G[B],
then w1 , v, w2 would be an induced P3 in the graph G \ Sj , which is impossible. This
means N (v) ∩ B is contained in some component C of G[B]. Furthermore, if v were to
have a neighbor w1 ∈ C and an non-neighbor w2 ∈ C, then v, w1 , w2 would be an induced


                                             180
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                    Chapter 4


P3 in the graph G \ Sj , which is impossible. Therefore, we either have N (v) ∩ B = ∅ or
N (v) ∩ B = C.
   For property (4), let u, v ∈ A \ T , and suppose (u, v) is a perennial edge. This means
u and v both belong to some cluster graph G \ Sj . If N (u) ∩ B = N (v) ∩ B = ∅, then
we are done, so suppose without loss of generality that N (u) ∩ B is nonempty. Let
w ∈ N (u) ∩ B, and suppose for a contradiction that N (u) ∩ B ̸= N (v) ∩ B. By property
(3), the path w, u, v is an induced P3 in the graph G \ Sj , which is a contradiction. Hence
property (4) holds.
   For property (5), let u, v ∈ A \ T , and suppose (u, v) is a perennial non-edge. As
before, we know u and v both belong to some cluster graph G \ Sj . Suppose for a
contradiction that N (u) ∩ B = N (v) ∩ B and N (u) ∩ B ̸= ∅. This means there is some
vertex w ∈ N (u) ∩ B. The path u, w, v is an induced P3 in the graph G \ Sj , which is a
contradiction. Therefore, property (5) holds.
   For the other direction, suppose properties (1)-(5) hold for G. Let i ∈ [p], and let
u, v, w ∈ V (G) \ Si . If u, v, w ∈ A, then these vertices (in any order) cannot form an
induced P3 since G[A] \ Si is a cluster graph. If u, v, w ∈ B, then these vertices cannot
form an induced P3 by property (2). Now suppose one of these vertices, say w, is in
A and the other two are in B. If u and v are adjacent, then they belong to the same
connected component of G[B]. In this case, by property (3), w is adjacent to either both
of the vertices u and v or neither, so these do not form an induced P3 . If u and v are
not adjacent, then they belong to distinct connected components of G[B], so by property
(3), these vertices cannot form an induced P3 .
   The last remaining possibility is that one of the three vertices, say w, is in B and the
other two are in A. Since T ⊆ Si , we know u, v ∈ A \ T . If u and v are adjacent, then
(u, v) is a perennial edge, so by property (4), these vertices cannot form an induced P3 .
If u and v are not adjacent, then (u, v) is a perennial non-edge, so properties (3) and (5)
                                            181
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                        Chapter 4


imply that these vertices cannot form an induced P3 . Hence G \ Si is a cluster graph.
   Let A, B, T be as defined in Lemma 77.


Lemma 78 Let n ∈ N, 0 ≤ k ≤ n, and let S1 , . . . , Sp be subsets of [n] of size at most k,
where 1 ≤ p ≤ (k + 1)3k . There is an algorithm that computes the number of graphs G
with vertex set [n] such that G \ Si is a cluster graph for all i ∈ [p]. The running time of
                       4 2k
this algorithm is 2O(k 3 ) n2 log n.


    Proof: Algorithm. Let A be the set of graphs GA with vertex set A such that
GA \ Si is a cluster graph for all i ∈ [p]. If B = ∅, then our algorithm returns |A|.
Otherwise, we proceed as follows.
   Suppose GA ∈ A. Let GP be the subgraph of GA with vertex set A \ T that contains
all of the perennial edges of GA , and no other edges. Let C be the set of all connected
components of GP . Let Γ1 (GA ) be the family of all subsets E ⊆ C with the following
property: for every connected component C of GP , if there exists a perennial non-edge
of GA with both endpoints in C, then C ∈ E.
   Suppose E ∈ Γ1 (GA ), and suppose P is a true partition of C \ E. We say P satisfies
property (5) if the following holds: for every perennial non-edge (u, v) in GA , if u and v
belong to different components of GP , say Cu and Cv , then either Cu and Cv belong to
different parts in P, or at least one of Cu , Cv belongs to E. Let Γ2 (GA , E) be the family
of all true partitions of C \ E that satisfy property (5). Let b = |B| and t = |T |. Our
algorithm returns
                         X X           X            X
                                                            (b′ )|P| S(b, b′ )2tb .
                        GA ∈A b′ ∈[b] E∈Γ1 (GA ) P∈Γ2 (GA ,E)

Here |P| is the number of parts in the partition P, (b′ )|P| := b′ (b′ − 1) · · · (b′ − |P| + 1)
denotes the falling factorial, and S(b, b′ ) stands for the number of ways of partitioning a
set of size b into b′ nonempty parts (a Stirling number of the second kind).

                                                  182
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                    Chapter 4


   Correctness. We show that this algorithm computes the number of graphs with
vertex set [n] that satisfy properties (1)-(5) from Lemma 77. The graph GA corresponds
to G[A], and b′ corresponds to the number of connected components in G[B]. By property
(4) of Lemma 77, vertices in the same component of GP must have the same neighborhood
in B. The set E corresponds to the set of components of GP whose neighborhood in B
is the empty set, and the partition P groups together components of GP that have the
same (nonempty) neighborhood in B. It is correct to only consider subsets E ⊆ C that
belong to Γ1 (GA ) because of properties (4) and (5) of Lemma 77. Similarly, we only
consider partitions P that belong to Γ2 (GA , E) because of property (5) of that lemma.
   The term (b′ )|P| counts the number of injective functions from the set of parts in P
to the set of connected components of G[B], which corresponds to choosing the neigh-
borhood of the components of GP that make up each part. The Stirling number S(b, b′ )
is equal to the number of ways of partitioning B into b′ nonempty cliques. Finally, there
are 2tb possibilities for the edges between A \ T and B.

   Running time. At the start of this algorithm, we first compute all of the necessary
Stirling numbers, which can be done in O(n2 ) time using the standard recurrences. We
have |A| ≤ (k 2 + k)3k , so the number of graphs with vertex set A is at most 2h(k) ≤
    4 2k                   2    k
2O(k 3 ) , where h(k) = (k +k)3
                             2
                                  . It is easy to check whether GA \ Si is a cluster graph
for each graph GA with vertex set A in O(n2 ) time. For the second summation, there are
                                                                             2    k
b ≤ n possible values of b′ . We also know |C| ≤ |A|, so there are at most 2(k +k)3 possible
                                3 k
values of E, and at most 3O(k 3 ) true partitions of C \ E. Each multiplication in the
summation over GA , b′ , E, P takes O(n2 log n) time since each number has at most O(n2 )
bits [91]. Computing t and b takes O(np) time, where p ≤ (k + 1)3k . Using repeated
squaring, we can compute 2tb in O(log n) multiplications, each of which takes O(n2 log n)
                                                         4 2k
time. Hence the running time of this algorithm is 2O(k 3 ) · n3 log n.

Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                             Chapter 4


    By Theorem 9, this gives us an algorithm that computes the number of yes-instances
                                                                             4 2k
of Cluster Vertex Deletion. The overall running time is 2O(k 3 ) · n3 log n.


4.3.5      The algorithm for GA

    Next, we design an algorithm for GA , the class of acyclic tournaments. For directed
graphs, perennial edges are defined in the same way as for undirected graphs. Suppose
G is a directed graph with vertex set V (G) ⊆ [n] for some n ∈ N, and suppose we have
been given a collection of subsets S1 , . . . , Sp of [n]. We say an edge (u, v) ∈ E(G) is
perennial if {u, v} ∩ Si = ∅ for some i ∈ [p].
    Every acyclic tournament has a unique topological ordering (see Section 4.2). If we
wish to add an additional vertex to an acyclic tournament G while still maintaining
the acyclic property, this vertex can either go between two consecutive vertices in the
topological ordering, or it can go before or after all other vertices of G. Let ≤ denote
the topological ordering of G, and suppose the vertices are ordered as follows: v1 ≤ v2 ≤
. . . ≤ vn , where n = |V (G)|. A gap λ in the topological ordering of G is either v1 , vn , or
a pair of consecutive vertices (vi , vi+1 ), where 1 ≤ i < n. If λ = v1 , we say the vertices
v1 , . . . , vn come after λ, and if λ = vn , we say the vertices v1 , . . . , vn come before λ. If
λ = (vi , vi+1 ), we say the vertices v1 , . . . , vi come before λ and the vertices vi+1 , . . . , vn
come after λ. When we say a gap in G, this is short for a gap in the topological ordering
of G.
   Suppose G is a tournament with vertex set [n] for some n ∈ N, and suppose we
have been given a collection of subsets S1 , . . . , Sp of [n], where p ≥ 1. Let A = pi=1 Si ,
                                                                                    S

B = [n] \ A, and T = pi=1 Si . For a vertex v ∈ A \ T , we say its neighborhood is
                         T

organized if there exists a gap λ in G[B] such that (u, v) ∈ E(G) for every u ∈ B that
comes before λ and (v, u) ∈ E(G) for every u ∈ B that comes after λ. Note that if such


                                                 184
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                         Chapter 4


a gap exists, it is unique. We refer to λ as the gap corresponding to v.
   Let ≤B denote the topological ordering of G[B], and suppose the vertices of B are
ordered as follows: u1 ≤B u2 ≤B . . . ≤B ub , where b = |B|. (In fact, these vertices are
distinct, so we also could have written u1 <B u2 , etc., but for simplicity, we just define
the relation ≤B ). Let B be the set of size b + 1 of all gaps in the topological ordering
of G[B]. The ordering of G[B] naturally leads to a total order on B, denoted by ⪯. We
have u1 ⪯ (u1 , u2 ) ⪯ (u2 , u3 ) ⪯ · · · ⪯ (ub−1 , ub ) ⪯ ub , where each of these elements is a
gap in G[B].
   Now suppose the neighborhood of v is organized for every v ∈ A \ T . For such a
graph G, let h : A \ T → B be the function defined by h(v) = λv for all v ∈ A \ T , where
λv is the gap corresponding to v. We call h the gap function of G. We say this function
is orderly if h(v) ⪯ h(w) for every perennial edge (v, w) in G[A].


Lemma 79 Let n ∈ N, and let S1 , . . . , Sp be subsets of [n], where p ≥ 1. Let A = pi=1 Si ,
                                                                                   S

B = [n] \ A, and T = pi=1 Si . Suppose G is a tournament with vertex set [n]. Then
                    T

G \ Si is acyclic for all i ∈ [p] if and only if G has the following properties:

  1. G[A] \ Si is acyclic for all i ∈ [p]

  2. G[B] is acyclic

  3. For every v ∈ A \ T , the neighborhood of v is organized

  4. The gap function h of G is orderly.


     Proof: First suppose G \ Si is acyclic for all i ∈ [p]. Property (1) is clearly true, and
we know G[B] is acyclic since G \ S1 is acyclic. For property (3), let v ∈ A \ T , which
means there is some j ∈ [r] such that v ∈
                                        / Sj . Let ≤B denote the topological ordering
of G[B], and suppose the elements of B are ordered as follows: u1 ≤B u2 ≤B . . . ≤B ub ,

                                              185
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                       Chapter 4


where b = |B|. If we have (v, u) ∈ E(G) for every u ∈ B, then let λ = u1 . Otherwise, let
u′ be the last vertex in the ordering u1 , . . . , ub such that (u′ , v) ∈ E(G). We claim that
(u, v) ∈ E(G) for all u ∈ B such that u ≤B u′ . Indeed, if there were a vertex u ̸= u′ in B
such that u ≤B u′ and (v, u) ∈ E(G), then v, u, u′ would be a cycle in G \ Sj . Therefore,
we have (u, v) ∈ E(G) for all u ∈ B such that u ≤B u′ , and we have (v, u) ∈ E(G) for all
u ̸= u′ in B such that u′ ≤B u. If u′ = ub , then let λ = u′ , and otherwise, let λ = (u′ , w),
where w is the vertex immediately after u′ in the ordering u1 , . . . , ub . This shows that
the neighborhood of v is organized, since λ is the gap corresponding to v.
   For property (4), suppose for a contradiction that h is not orderly. This means there
is some perennial edge (v, w) in G[A] such that h(v) ̸⪯ h(w), so we have h(w) ⪯ h(v)
and h(v) ̸= h(w). Hence there is some u ∈ B that comes after h(w) and comes before
h(v). We know v and w belong to some acyclic graph G \ Sj . Therefore, u, v, w is a cycle
in G \ Sj , which is a contradiction.
   For the other direction, suppose properties (1)-(4) hold for G. Let i ∈ [p]. We show
that G \ Si has a topological ordering. The graph G[A] \ Si has a topological ordering by
property (1) (denoted by ≤A ), and the graph G[B] has a topological ordering by property
(2) (denoted by ≤B ). Based on these, we can define the relation ≤ on V (G) \ Si in the
following way. Let v, w ∈ V (G) \ Si . If v, w ∈ A, then v ≤ w if v ≤A w, and w ≤ v if
w ≤A v. If v, w ∈ B, then v ≤ w if v ≤B w, and w ≤ v if w ≤B v. If v ∈ A and w ∈ B,
then v ≤ w if w comes after h(v), and w ≤ v if w comes before h(v).
   To see that the relation ≤ is a topological ordering, we first check that it is a total
order. Clearly, this relation is reflexive. We cannot have both v ≤ w and w ≤ v in the
case when v ∈ A and w ∈ B, so it is also antisymmetric. To see that it is transitive,
suppose u ≤ v and v ≤ w for some u, v, w ∈ V (G) \ Si . If u, v, w ∈ A or u, v, w ∈ B, then
clearly u ≤ w. If u ∈ A and v, w ∈ B, then we have u ≤ w since w comes after h(u). If
v ∈ A and u, w ∈ B, or if w ∈ A and u, v ∈ B, then we can similarly see that u ≤ w. If
                                             186
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                   Chapter 4


u ∈ B and v, w ∈ A, then we have u ≤ w since h(v) ⪯ h(w) by property (4). If v ∈ B
and u, w ∈ A, or if w ∈ B and u, v ∈ A, then we can similarly see that u ≤ w. Thus the
relation ≤ is transitive.
   Now we just need to check that v ≤ w for every edge (v, w) in the graph G \ Si .
Suppose (v, w) is an edge in G \ Si . If v, w ∈ A, then clearly v ≤ w since v ≤A w, and
similarly, we are done if v, w ∈ B. If v ∈ A and w ∈ B, then w comes after h(v) by
property (3), so we have v ≤ w. Lastly, if v ∈ B and w ∈ A, then v comes before h(w) by
property (3), so we again have v ≤ w. Therefore, the relation ≤ is a topological ordering,
and thus G \ Si is acyclic.
   Let A, B, T be as defined in Lemma 79.


Lemma 80 Let n ∈ N, 0 ≤ k ≤ n, and let S1 , . . . , Sp be subsets of [n] of size at most k,

4.3.6        The algorithm for GS

Split graph properties

   If C is the always-clique set, I is the always-independent set, and Q is the questioning
set of a split graph G, then (C, I, Q) is called the split decomposition of G. More generally,
a decomposition of a graph G is a triple (A1 , A2 , A3 ) such that {A1 , A2 , A3 } is a partition of
V (G). The following lemma is a more compact version of the results proved in Chapter 2
(see Observation 6 and Lemmas 25 and 26).


Lemma 81 The split decomposition (C, I, Q) of a split graph G has the following prop-
erties:

                                                189
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                            Chapter 4


   1. C is a clique and I is an independent set

   2. Q is either a clique or an independent set

   3. Every vertex in Q is adjacent to every vertex in C and is non-adjacent to every
      vertex in I

   4. If Q is a clique, then every vertex in C has a neighbor in I

   5. If Q is an independent set, then every vertex in I has a non-neighbor in C.


    The converse is also true, as we can see in the following lemma, which is a compact
version of Lemmas 27 and 30 from Chapter 2.


Lemma 82 Suppose G is a graph, and suppose (C, I, Q) is a decomposition of G that
satisfies properties (1)-(5) from Lemma 81. Then G is a split graph, and furthermore,
(C, I, Q) is the split decomposition of G.


    We will also need two more properties of split decompositions.


Lemma 83 Let G be a split graph. If u, v ∈ V (G) are true or false twins, then they
belong to the same part in the split decomposition (C, I, Q) of G. In other words, we
have u, v ∈ C, u, v ∈ I, or u, v ∈ Q.


     Proof: Suppose u ∈ C ′ for some split partition (C ′ , I ′ ) of G. We claim that in this
case, there must also be a split partition (C ⋆ , I ⋆ ) of G such that v ∈ C ⋆ . If v ∈ C ′ , then we
are done. Otherwise, we have u ∈ C ′ and v ∈ I ′ , which means (C ′ \{u}∪{v}, I ′ \{v}∪{u})
is a split partition of G that places v in the clique. Similarly, we can see that if u belongs
to the independent set of some split partition of G, then the same is true for v. Therefore,
we have u, v ∈ C, u, v ∈ I, or u, v ∈ Q.

                                                190
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                            Chapter 4


Lemma 84 Suppose G is a split graph with an induced subgraph Ĝ. Let (C, I, Q) be
the split decomposition of G, and let (Ĉ, ˆ
                                           I, Q̂) be the split decomposition of Ĝ. We have
Ĉ ⊆ C and Iˆ ⊆ I.


     Proof: To see that Ĉ ⊆ C, suppose v ∈ V (G) \ C. This means there is some split
partition (C ′ , I ′ ) of G such that v ∈ I ′ . Let C ⋆ = C ′ ∩ V (Ĝ) and I ⋆ = I ′ ∩ V (Ĝ). If
v ∈ V (Ĝ), then the pair (C ⋆ , I ⋆ ) is a split partition of Ĝ with the property that v ∈ I ⋆ , so
v∈
 / Ĉ. Otherwise, if v ∈
                       / V (Ĝ), then clearly v ∈
                                                / Ĉ. Hence Ĉ ⊆ C. A similar argument
(reversing the roles of the always-clique set and the always-independent set) shows that
Iˆ ⊆ I.


Guess lists

    The goal of this section (Section 4.3.6) is to prove the following lemma.


Lemma 85 Let n ∈ N, 0 ≤ k ≤ n, and let S1 , . . . , Sp be subsets of [n] of size at most k,
where 1 ≤ p ≤ (k + 1)5k . There is an algorithm that computes the number of graphs G
with vertex set [n] such that G \ Si is a split graph for all i ∈ [p]. The running time of
                     O(k2 5k )
this algorithm is 42             · n7 log n.


    In this algorithm, we will guess certain information about these graphs in order to
group them into smaller families of graphs that are easier to count. Suppose we have
been given n ∈ N, as well as a collection of subsets S1 , . . . , Sp of [n], where p ≥ 1. Let
A = pi=1 Si , and let B = [n] \ A.
    S

    Suppose G is a graph with vertex set [n] such that G \ Si is a split graph for all
i ∈ [p]. Let (CB , IB , QB ) be the split decomposition of G[B], and let (Ci , Ii , Qi ) be the
split decomposition of G \ Si for each i ∈ [p]. For such a graph G, we define the sets
CByes , CBno , IByes , IBno in the following way. Let CByes be the set of vertices in CB that have

                                                191
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                                             Chapter 4


a neighbor in IB , and let CBno be the set of vertices in CB that do not have a neighbor
in IB . Let IByes be the set of vertices in IB that have a non-neighbor in CB , and let IBno
be the set of vertices in IB that do not have a non-neighbor in CB . In Definition 10, the
identifiers C, I, and Q stand for the parts in the split decomposition of G \ Si , D stands
for “deleted,” and E stands for “empty set.”

Definition 10 A guess list is a tuple

        (i)

of CByes , CBno , IByes , IBno , QB , respectively, and z indicates whether or not QB is a clique. For
A′ ⊆ A, the values fC (A′ ), fI (A′ ), and fQ (A′ ) specify the number of vertices in certain
sets (namely CBno , IBno , and QB ) whose neighborhood in A is equal to A′ . Each of these
numbers is either 0, 1, or at least 2. When we say fC (A′ ) matches with a number ℓ, this
means ℓ = 0 if fC (A′ ) = 0, ℓ = 1 if fC (A′ ) = 1, and ℓ ≥ 2 if fC (A′ ) = 2. We also define
matches with in the same way for fI (A′ ) and fQ (A′ ).

   If Qi is a clique, then we also need to make sure every vertex in Ci ∩ A has a neighbor
in Ii . Similarly, if Qi is an independent set, then we need to make sure every vertex in
Ii ∩ A has a non-neighbor in Ci . The edges between Ci ∩ A and Ii (resp. Ii ∩ A and Ci ) are
not completely specified by L since we do not guess information about the neighborhoods
of vertices in IByes (resp. CByes ). However, we can obtain two more necessary conditions
for L.
                                                              (i)
   Fix i ∈ [p]. We say a vertex v ∈ A such that gA (v) = C is satisfied for i if at least
one of the following holds:

                                                  (i)
   • In GA , v has a neighbor in {u ∈ A : gA (u) = I}

                                                                    (i)
   • v ∈ A′ for some A′ ⊆ A such that fI (A′ ) > 0 or gQ (A′ ) = I.

In other words, v is satisfied if it has a neighbor in Ii that comes from A, IBno , or QB (in
                                                                                   (i)
the graphs that conform to L). Similarly, we say a vertex v ∈ A such that gA (v) = I is
satisfied for i if at least one of the following holds:
