                 Tp                                                                    (i)
   Let T =        i=1 Si . A vertex v ∈ A\T is a universally clique vertex if we have gA (v) = C

or v ∈ Si for all i ∈ [p]. A vertex v ∈ A \ T is a universally independent vertex if we
         (i)
have gA (v) = I or v ∈ Si for all i ∈ [p]. For a vertex v ∈ A \ T , if there is some i ∈ [p]
                                               198
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                        Chapter 4

                                       (i)
such that L is of clique type for i, gA (v) = C, and v is not satisfied for i, then we say v
is neighbor needy. Similarly, if there is some i ∈ [p] such that L is of independent type
        (i)
for i, gA (v) = I, and v is not satisfied for i, then we say v is non-neighbor needy. We
say L is globally valid if the following holds:

    • For all v ∈ A \ T , if v is neighbor needy, then v is a universally clique vertex

    • For all v ∈ A \ T , if v is non-neighbor needy, then v is a universally independent
      vertex.

    Putting this all together, we say the guess list L is valid if it is consistent, locally
valid, and globally valid.


The algorithm

    As part of our algorithm, we will need to compute the number of ordered partitions
of CBno , IBno , and QB that are consistent with our guesses for the sizes of the parts. For
a nonnegative integer j and an ordered list A1 , . . . , Ar of subsets of {0, 1, . . . , n}, let
pB (j, F) denote the number of ordered partitions of [j] into r parts P1 , . . . , Pr such that
|Pi | ∈ Ai for all i ∈ [r].
    We will also need to compute the number of possibilities for G[B]. For a split graph G
with split decomposition (C, I, Q), let Cyes be the set of vertices in C that have a neighbor
in I, and let Cno be the set of vertices in C that do not have a neighbor in I. Let Iyes be
the set of vertices in I that have a non-neighbor in C, and let Ino be the set of vertices in
I that do not have a non-neighbor in C. For nonnegative integers cyes , cno , iyes , ino , q and




                                              199
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                                              Chapter 4


a boolean z, let sB (cyes , cno , iyes , ino , q, z) denote the number of split graphs G such that

                 Cyes = [cyes ],

                  Cno = [cyes + 1, cyes + cno ],

                  Iyes = [cyes + cno + 1, cyes + cno + iyes ],

                   Ino = [cyes + cno + iyes + 1, cyes + cno + iyes + ino ],

                    Q = [cyes + cno + iyes + ino + 1, cyes + cno + iyes + ino + q],

and such that Q is a clique if and only if z = 1.
    For now, we assume we have algorithms that compute sB (cyes , cno , iyes , ino , q, z) and
pB (j, F). See the following two sections for the details of these algorithms. Suppose we
have been given a fixed valid guess list

                                                                      (1)         (p)    (1)         (p)
            L = (GA , cyes , cno , iyes , ino , q, z, fC , fI , fQ , gA , . . . , gA , gQ , . . . , gQ ).

Let ĉ be the number of vertices in A \ T that are neighbor needy, and let č be the number
of universally clique vertices in A \ T that are not neighbor needy. Let î be the number
of vertices in A \ T that are non-neighbor needy, and let ǐ be the number of universally
independent vertices in A \ T that are not non-neighbor needy.
    We define the set families FC , FI , and FQ in the following way. Let A′1 , . . . , A′r be all

Counting ordered partitions

    For a nonnegative integer j and an ordered list A1 , . . . , Ar of subsets of {0, 1, . . . , n},
we defined pB (j, F) as the number of ordered partitions of [j] into r parts P1 , . . . , Pr such
that |Pi | ∈ Ai for all i ∈ [r]. We now describe how to compute this function.
    Suppose we have been given j and A1 , . . . , Ar . For 0 ≤ s ≤ j, 1 ≤ t ≤ r, let p(s, t)
be the number of ordered partitions of [s] into t parts P1 , . . . , Pt such that |Pi | ∈ Ai for
all i ∈ [t]. It is easy to verify that this satisfies the recurrence

                                              X s
                                    p(s, t) =       p(s − a, t − 1).
                                              a∈A
                                                  a
                                                    t
                                                a≤s


For the base case, we have p(s, 0) = 1 for all 0 ≤ s ≤ j. In the end, we compute
pB (j, F) = p(j, r) using dynamic programming.


Counting split graphs

    Next, we describe how to compute sB (cyes , cno , iyes , ino , q, z), which corresponds to the
number of possibilities for G[B]. Suppose we have been given cyes , cno , iyes , ino , q, and z.
If z = 1, then we return 0 if cno > 0. If q ≤ 1 or z = 0 (i.e., if QB is an independent set),
then we return 0 if ino > 0. Otherwise, we proceed as follows.
    We just need to compute the number of possibilities for the edges between Cyes and
Iyes , since there is only one possibility for all other edges. For v ∈ [cyes ], let Gv be the set

Proof of correctness

    First, we show that every graph we wish to count conforms to a unique guess list.

Lemma 86 Suppose G is a graph with vertex set [n] such that G \ Si is a split graph for
all i ∈ [p]. Then there is a unique guess list L such that G conforms to L.

     Proof: Let GA = G[A], cyes = |CByes |, cno = |CBno |, iyes = |IByes |, ino = |IBno | and
q = |QB |. Let z = 1 if QB is a clique, and let z = 0 otherwise. For each A′ ⊆ A, we
define fC (A′ ), fI (A′ ), and fQ (A′ ) in the natural way. Let fˆC (A′ ) (resp. fˆI (A′ )) be the
number of vertices v ∈ CBno (resp. v ∈ IBno ) such that N (v) ∩ A = A′ . If fˆC (A′ ) ≤ 1, then
let fC (A′ ) = fˆC (A′ ), and otherwise, let fC (A′ ) = 2. Similarly, if fˆI (A′ ) ≤ 1, then let
fI (A′ ) = fˆI (A′ ), and otherwise, let fI (A′ ) = 2. If |N (A′ )| ≤ 1, then let fQ (A′ ) = |N (A′ )|,
and otherwise, let fQ (A′ ) = 2.
                                                             (i)      (i)
    Next, for each i ∈ [p], we define the functions gA and gQ in the following way. For
                    (i)                            (i)                          (i)
each v ∈ A, let gA (v) = D if v ∈ Si , let gA (v) = C if v ∈ Ci , let gA (v) = I if v ∈ Ii ,
                                                  202

Lemma 87 Suppose G is a graph with vertex set [n] such that G \ Si is a split graph for
all i ∈ [p]. If L is the guess list from Lemma 86 such that G conforms to L, then L is
valid.

        Proof: Clearly L is consistent. By the definition of clique type, we know L is of
clique type for i if and only if Qi is a clique, for each i ∈ [p]. Since G \ Si is a split
graph, this means G \ Si satisfies all five properties of Lemma 81 for all i ∈ [p], so L
is locally valid. To see that L is globally valid, suppose there is a vertex v ∈ A \ T
that is neighbor needy, i.e., there is some i ∈ [p] for which property (4) of Lemma 81
implies that v has a neighbor in IByes . If v ∈ Ii ∪ Qi for some i ∈ [p], then Lemma 81
implies that v is non-adjacent to every vertex in IByes , since IByes ⊆ Ii by Lemma 84. This
a contradiction, so v must be a universally clique vertex. Similarly, if there is a vertex
v ∈ A \ T that is non-neighbor needy, then v must be a universally independent vertex.
Hence L is globally valid, which means L is valid.
       Finally, we show that we compute the correct number of graphs for each valid guess
list.

Running time

    Each of the necessary multinomial coefficients can be computed in O(n) time. When
we sum over L ∈ L, the number of possible functions in the entries of L is dominated by

4.4        Faster Algorithm for Counting Graphs with a

           Small Vertex Cover

    In the previous section, we designed algorithms for counting yes-instances of four
vertex deletion problems, including Vertex Cover. However, the running time of
                                                              205
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                   Chapter 4


each of these algorithms was a double-exponential or triple-exponential function of k. In
                                                                                    4 2k
particular, the running time for counting graphs with a small vertex cover was 2O(k 2 ) ·
n2 log2 n. In this section, we show that using ideas inspired by the Buss kernel for this
problem, we can design a much faster algorithm for counting yes-instances of Vertex
Cover.


Theorem 11 There is an algorithm that given n ∈ N and 0 ≤ k ≤ n, computes the
number of labeled graphs with vertex set [n] that have a vertex cover of size at most k.
                         2
This algorithm uses k O(k ) n3 arithmetic operations.


   To design this algorithm, we first observe that the vertices of each graph can be
partitioned into three parts, H, I, and R, defined as follows.


Definition 12 The high-degree set of a graph G is the set H := {v ∈ V (G) : deg(v) >
k}. The isolated set of G is the set I := {v ∈ V (G) \ H : N (v) ⊆ H}. The remaining
set of G is the set R := V (G) \ (H ∪ I).


   If G has a vertex cover of size at most k, then the sizes of H and R can be bounded
above by a function of k.


Lemma 89 If G is a graph with a vertex cover of size at most k, then the high-degree
set satisfies |H| ≤ k, the remaining set satisfies |R| ≤ k 2 + k, and G[R] has at most k 2
edges.


    Proof: Let v ∈ H, and suppose S is a vertex cover of G of size at most k. We know
v ∈ S because otherwise all neighbors of v would belong to S, which is impossible since
|S| ≤ k. Therefore, we have |H| ≤ |S| ≤ k.
   The bound on the size of R follows from the standard Buss kernelization algorithm
for Vertex Cover. Consider the following two reduction rules: (1) If G contains an
                                            206
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                    Chapter 4


isolated vertex v, then delete v from G; (2) If G contains a vertex v of degree more than
k, then delete v from G and decrease k by 1. If we apply these rules exhaustively to a
graph with a vertex cover of size at most k, then the resulting graph has at most k 2 + k
vertices and at most k 2 edges [94].
    Let G be a graph with a vertex cover of size at most k, and let H, I, and R be the
high-degree set, the isolated set, and the remaining set of G, respectively. Suppose we
exhaustively apply the above reduction rules to G. Let H ′ be the set of vertices deleted
by reduction rule (2), let I ′ be the set of vertices deleted by reduction rule (1), and let
R′ = V (G) \ (H ′ ∪ I ′ ).
    We know G[R′ ] has at most k 2 + k vertices and at most k 2 edges, and we claim that
R ⊆ R′ . We have H ′ ⊆ H since the degree of a vertex can only decrease when we delete
other vertices. To see that I ′ ⊆ I, let v ∈ I ′ . We first check that v ∈
                                                                         / H. If v ∈ H, then
for v to become isolated, all of its neighbors would need to be deleted by reduction rule
(2). However, that would mean G has more than k vertices of degree more than k, which
is impossible since G has a vertex cover of size at most k. To see that N (v) ⊆ H, we
first observe that N (v) ⊆ H ′ ∪ I ′ since v is isolated at the moment when it is deleted.
No two vertices in I ′ can be adjacent because if they were, then neither one would ever
be isolated. This means N (v) is in fact contained in H ′ ⊆ H, so v ∈ I. Thus we have
I ′ ⊆ I, so R′ ⊇ R as desired.
    Algorithm overview. We consider all possibilities for the sizes of H, I, and R that
could occur in an n-vertex graph G with a vertex cover of size at most k. Suppose we
have guessed that |H| = h, |I| = i, and |R| = r, where h + i + r = n. We will describe
how to count the number of graphs with H = [h], I = [h + 1, h + i], and R = [h + i + 1, n].
To consider all possibilities for the label set of these three subgraphs, we then multiply
this result by nh n−h
                     
                    i
                       .
                       h
    We loop over all 2(2) possibilities for the edges of G[H], and for each of these, we
                                            207
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                    Chapter 4


proceed as follows. For each vertex v ∈ H, we consider all possibilities for its degree into
I and R, i.e., we guess the values of |N (v) ∩ I| and |N (v) ∩ R|. Degree values that are
greater than k (and some others) can all be bucketed into a single category. For each of
these guesses, we compute the number of possibilities for G[H ∪ I] and G[H ∪ R] and
then multiply those two results.
   The subgraph G[H ∪ I]: There are no edges in G[I] since I is an independent set, so
we just need to count the possibilities for the edges between H and I. If we have guessed
that the vertex v ∈ H has |N (v) ∩ I| = dv , then there are div possibilities for N (v) ∩ I.
                                                               

Even if we just have a lower bound, e.g., we have guessed that |N (v) ∩ I| > k, it is easy
to compute the number of possibilities for N (v) ∩ I in a similar way.
   The subgraph G[H ∪ R]: We loop over all possibilities for the edges of G[R], knowing
that this subgraph has at most k 2 + k vertices and at most k 2 edges. In this loop, we
only consider subgraphs G[R] such that all vertices have degree at least 1 and such that
G[R] has a vertex cover of size at most k − h. Suppose we have a particular guess for
G[R] in mind, as well as a guess for G[H] from above. What remains now is to count
the possibilities for the edges between H and R. For each vertex v ∈ H, we have already
guessed the degree of v into R. For each vertex v ∈ R, the degree of v into H must be
at most k − dv , where dv is the degree of v in G[R], since v ∈
                                                              / H. Using this information
about the degrees, we run a divide and conquer algorithm to compute the number of
possibilities for G[H ∪ R].


4.4.1     Algorithm for Theorem 11

   The following procedures describe the algorithm for Theorem 11 in detail. To run
the algorithm, one would call Count VC Graphs(n, k).
   We refer to h-dimensional vectors as h-tuples, where h is a nonnegative integer. For

bound (e.g., ≥ k + 1). To distinguish between these two types of guesses, we use an
h-tuple of booleans eH = (e1 , . . . , eh ): the guess for v ∈ [h] is an exact number if and
only if ev = true. This is will be used in the function Count HR(k, i, r, dH ).

4.4.2        Proof of correctness

    Suppose we have been given n and k as input. To prove correctness of the algorithm
above, we need to show that Count VC Graphs(n, k) returns the number of graphs
with vertex set [n] that have a vertex cover of size at most k. We work our way up
through each of the procedures, starting with Div Conq.
                                                   210
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                                   Chapter 4


   We say the arguments (dH , dR , eH , s, t) are in the domain of Div Conq if they have
the following properties for some (h, i, r) ∈ I: The h-tuples dH = (d1 , . . . , dh ) and
dR = (d′1 , . . . , d′r ) are such that 0 ≤ dv ≤ k + 1 for all v ∈ [h] and 0 ≤ d′v < k for all
v ∈ [r], and eH is an h-tuple of booleans. We also require s, t ∈ [r] and t ≥ s − 1.
   We say the arguments (dH , dR , eH , s, t) come from (h, i, r) if the above properties
hold for this particular triple (h, i, r) ∈ I. For some intuition, this is true when the
calculations that we are currently doing come from calling Count HIR(k, h, i, r) in
Count VC Graphs.
   Let (h, i, r) ∈ I be fixed. Suppose dH = (d1 , . . . , dh ) and dR = (d′1 , . . . , d′r ) are such
that 0 ≤ dv ≤ k + 1 for all v ∈ [h] and 0 ≤ d′v < k for all v ∈ [r]. Also, suppose eH is an
h-tuple of booleans. Let H = [h], and let R = [h + i + 1, h + i + r]. For a bipartite graph
GB with bipartition (H, R′ ), where R′ ⊆ R, we say GB has degrees given by (dH , dR , eH )
if GB has the following properties:

   • for every v ∈ H such that ev = true, we have deg(v) = dv

   • for every v ∈ H such that ev = false, we have deg(v) ≥ dv

   • for every h + i + v ∈ R′ , we have deg(h + i + v) ≤ d′v .

Lemma 91 Let GH be a graph with vertex set H, and suppose dH = (d1 , . . . , dh ) is such
that 0 ≤ dv ≤ k + 1 for all v ∈ H. The procedure Count HR(k, i, r, dH ) computes the
number of graphs G with vertex set H ∪ R such that G[H] = GH , |N (v) ∩ R| ≥ dv for all
v ∈ H, G[R] has at most k 2 edges, degG (v) ≤ k and degG[R] (v) ≥ 1 for all v ∈ R, and
G[R] has a vertex cover of size at most k − h.

that has degree dv in the induced subgraph G[R], requiring that |N (v) ∩ H| ≤ k − dv is

defined as in Count HR. To see that the arguments (dH , dGR , eH , 1, r) are in the domain
of Div Conq, we just need to check that 0 ≤ d′v < k for all v ∈ [r]. This is true since
1 ≤ degR (v) ≤ k for all v ∈ V (GR ) by the definition of R. Therefore, by Lemma 90, is

   Let H = [h], and let I = [h + 1, h + i]. In the next lemma, we show that Count HI

Lemma 92 Let GH be a graph with vertex set H, and suppose dH = (d1 , . . . , dh ) is such
that 0 ≤ dv ≤ k + 1 − degGH (v) for all v ∈ H. The procedure Count HI(k, i, r, dH )

if dv ≤ k − degGH (v), and |N (v) ∩ I| ≥ dv if dv = k + 1 − degGH (v).


    Proof: Since G[H] and G[I] are fixed, we just need to compute the number of

possibilities for N (v) ∩ I for each vertex v ∈ H. If dv ≤ k − degGH (v), then the number

   For Count HIR, we first need a way to characterize the high-degree set, isolated
set, and remaining set of the graph.


Lemma 93 Let G be a graph with vertex set [n]. Suppose H, I, and R are disjoint
subsets of [n] such that H ∪ I ∪ R = [n] and |H| ≤ k. Then G has a vertex cover of size
at most k and H is the high-degree set, I is the isolated set, and R is the remaining set
of G if and only if these sets have the following properties:

  1. deg(v) > k for all v ∈ H

  2. I is an independent set

  3. degG (v) ≤ k for all v ∈ R

  4. degG[R] (v) ≥ 1 for all v ∈ R

  5. G[R] has a vertex cover of size at most k − |H|

  6. G has no edges with one endpoint in I and the other in R

  7. G[R] has at most k 2 edges.


    Proof: For the forward direction, suppose G has a vertex cover of size at most k
and H is the high-degree set, I is the isolated set, and R is the remaining set of G.
Properties (1), (3), and (6) follow immediately from the definitions of the high-degree
set, the isolated set, and the remaining set. Property (2) holds because if there were a
vertex v ∈ I with a neighbor in I, then we would not have N (v) ⊆ H. Property (4)
holds because if there were a vertex v ∈ R with degG[R] (v) = 0, then v would actually
belong to I. For property (5), suppose S is a vertex cover of G of size at most k. We
                                            215
Counting Yes-Instances of Vertex Deletion Problems in FPT Time                          Chapter 4


know H ⊆ S, so S \ (H ∪ I) is a vertex cover of G[R] of size at most k − |H|. Property
(7) follows from Lemma 89.
   For the other direction, suppose properties (1)-(7) hold. Let e

more than k since |H| ≤ k. Hence I ⊆ e

To see that G has a vertex cover of size at most k, let S be vertex cover of G[R] of size
at most k − |H|. The set S ∪ H is a vertex cover of G since I is an independent set, and
we have |S ∪ H| ≤ k.


Lemma 94 Let (h, i, r) ∈ I, H = [h], I = [h + 1, h + i], and R = [h + i + 1, n]. The
procedure Count HIR(k, h, i, r) computes the number of graphs G with vertex set [n]
that have a vertex cover of size at most k and have high-degree set H, isolated set I, and
remaining set R.


     Proof: In Count HIR, we sum over GH ∈ H to consider all possiblities for G[H].
For each v ∈ H, the value dIv is a guess for |N (v)∩I|. If dIv ≤ k −degGH (v), then our guess
is that |N (v) ∩ I| = dIv ; if dIv = k + 1 − degGH (v), then our guess is that |N (v) ∩ I| ≥ dIv .
Similarly, each value dR
                       v is a guess for |N (v) ∩ R|. Every one of these is a lower bound,

i.e. our guess is that |N (v) ∩ R| ≥ dR                                             I      R
                                      v . The set DGH covers all possibilities for dv and dv

in a graph such that deg(v) > k for all v ∈ H. Therefore, by Lemmas 91 and 92, the
summation returned by Count HIR computes the number of graphs with vertex set [n]
such that H, I, and R satisfy properties (1)-(7) from Lemma 93. By that lemma, this
is equal to the number of graphs with vertex set [n] that have a vertex cover of size at
most k and have high-degree set H, isolated set I, and remaining set R.

   For Count VC Graphs, we need to show that the number of graphs does not
depend on the particular choices of the labels in each of the sets H, I, and R. Suppose
A = {a1 , . . . , ar } and B = {b1 , . . . , br } are finite subsets of N such that |A| = |B|, where
the elements ai and bi are listed in increasing order. We define ϕ(A, B) : A → B as the
bijection that maps ai to bi for all i ∈ [r].


Lemma 95 Let (h, i, r) ∈ I. Let H = [h], I = [h + 1, h + i], and R = [h + i + 1, n].
Suppose H ′ , I ′ , and R′ are disjoint subsets of [n] such that H ′ ∪ I ′ ∪ R′ = [n], |H ′ | = h,
|I ′ | = i, and |R′ | = r. Let Gn,k be the set of graphs with vertex set [n] that have a vertex
cover of size at most k. Let G be the set of graphs in Gn,k that have high-degree set
H, isolated set I, and remaining set R. Also, let G ′ be the set of graphs Gn,k that have
high-degree set H ′ , isolated set I ′ , and remaining set R′ . Then |G| = |G ′ |.


     Proof: Let f : G → G ′ be the bijection defined as follows: for each G ∈ G, f (G) is the
graph obtained by applying ϕ(H, H ′ ) to the labels in H, applying ϕ(I, I ′ ) to the labels
in I, and applying ϕ(R, R′ ) to the labels in R. The map f is a bijection since each of the
relabeling functions is a bijection, so |G| = |G ′ |.


Lemma 96 The procedure Count VC Graphs(n, k) computes the number of graphs
G with vertex set [n] that have a vertex cover of size at most k.


     Proof: Let G be such a graph, and let H, I and R be the high-degree set, isolated
set, and remaining set, respectively. We have |H| ≤ k and |R| ≤ k 2 + k by Lemma 89,
so I indeed covers all possible values of h = |H|, i = |I|, r = |R|.

4.4.3     Running time

   All of the running times that we discuss here are measured in terms of the number of
arithmetic operations. At the start of this algorithm (Theorem 11), we first compute all
of the necessary binomial coefficients. In particular, we compute all values of ab such
                                                                                  

that 0 ≤ a ≤ n and 0 ≤ b ≤ a, which can be done in O(n2 ) time.
   For ℓ ≥ 0, let T (ℓ) be the maximum running time of Div Conq(dH , dR , eH , s, t)
across all values of (dH , dR , eH , s, t) in the domain such that ℓ = t − s + 1. The base case
of Div Conq takes O(k) time. We have |D| ≤ (k + 2)k · 2k since h ≤ k, which gives us
the recurrence
                                                               
                   T (ℓ) = (k + 2)k · 2k · T (⌈ℓ/2⌉) + T (⌊ℓ/2⌋) + O(1).

By standard arguments such as the master theorem, we can see that T (ℓ) = ℓO(k log(k)) .
   For Count HR, suppose the arguments are (k, i, r, dH ). We have

we first build the set of all graphs with vertex set {h + i + 1, . . . , h + i + r} and at most
k 2 edges (and the correct degrees), and then for each such graph, we check if it has a
vertex cover of size at most k − h, which can be done in time O(2k n2 ) using the standard

branching algorithm. Calling Div Conq with s = 1 and t = r takes rO(k log k) = k O(k log k)
                                                                 2
time, so the running time of Count HR(k, i, r, dH ) is k O(k ) n2 .
   It is easy to see that the running time of Count HI(k, i, GH , dH ) is O(hn) = O(kn).

                                            219
Chapter 5

Concluding Remarks and Open
Problems

Interval graphs and permutation graphs are two graph classes with a relatively simple
structure for which there might be efficient counting and sampling algorithms. These
problems are semi-open, in the sense that they might be open, but it might also be the
case that polynomial-time algorithms follow from other results about these graph classes
that are phrased in different language (e.g., generating function results). The next step
for these two graph classes would be to analyze what is known (see Section 1.2) and
determine whether polynomial-time algorithms follow from these results.
   There are many graph classes for which the problems of efficient counting and sam-
pling are fully open including graphs of bounded degeneracy, graphs of bounded treewidth,
chordal bipartite graphs, distance-hereditary graphs, perfect graphs, strongly chordal
graphs, and weakly chordal graphs. These problems are also open for many other graph
classes characterized by a finite set of forbidden minors, subgraphs, or induced subgraphs.
It is worth noting that for some well-known graph classes of this form, such as planar
graphs, polynomial-time algorithms are known [23, 24]. In the next few sections, we

                                           220
Concluding Remarks and Open Problems                                            Chapter 5


discuss open problems that follow naturally from our results.



5.1     Labeled Chordal Graphs

   In Chapter 2, we designed algorithms for exact counting and uniform sampling of
labeled chordal graphs that use O(n7 ) arithmetic operations (i.e., in O(n9 log n) time in
the RAM model). A natural open problem is to design a substantially faster algorithm
for exact counting or uniform sampling of chordal graphs. We also designed approx-
imate counting and approximate sampling algorithms for labeled chordal graphs that
run in O(n3 log n log7 (1/ε)) deterministic time and O(n3 log n log7 (1/ε)) expected time,
respectively. Both of these algorithms are quite fast. However, one could argue that our
approximate sampling algorithm is unsatisfactory in the sense that it can never output
a non-split chordal graph. Therefore, another interesting open problem would be to ask
for an approximate sampling algorithm with a stronger guarantee: namely, a sampling
algorithm such that for every chordal graph G, we have (1 − ε)p ≤ Pr(G) ≤ (1 + ε)p,
where p is the probability given by the uniform distribution over all chordal graphs and
Pr(G) is the probability that the approximate sampling algorithm outputs the graph G.
Two interesting approaches to consider for this are Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithms
and the Boltzmann sampling scheme used in [24] for planar graphs.



5.2     Unlabeled Chordal Graphs

   In Chapter 3, we built upon the algorithm of Wormald for generating random unla-
beled graphs to design an algorithm that, given n, generates a random unlabeled chordal
graph on n vertices in expected polynomial time. This serves as a proof of concept that
one can obtain a sampling algorithm for unlabeled graphs from a GI-complete graph class

                                           221
Concluding Remarks and Open Problems                                           Chapter 5


G using the following two ingredients: (1) an FPT algorithm for counting labeled graphs
in G with a given automorphism π parameterized by the number of moved points of π and
(2) a bound on the probability that a labeled graph in G has a given automorphism. A few
potential candidates for this are bipartite graphs, strongly chordal graphs, and chordal
bipartite graphs, all of which are GI-complete. An additional open problem would be to
design a uniform, or approximately uniform, sampling algorithm ––– either for unlabeled
chordal graphs or general unlabeled graphs ––– that runs in expected polynomial time
even when we condition on the output graph.



5.3      Yes-Instances of Parameterized Vertex Deletion

         Problems

   In Chapter 4, we designed algorithms for counting labeled yes-instances of several
vertex deletion problems: Vertex Cover, Cluster Vertex Deletion, Feedback
Vertex Set in Tournaments, and Split Vertex Deletion. Our best algorithm
                                                        2
for counting yes-instances of Vertex Cover uses k O(k ) n3 arithmetic operations, and
the other three counting algorithms have running times that are double exponential in
k, the number of vertices deleted. From the first of these, we can also derive a sam-
                                                                                    2
pling algorithm that generates a random yes-instance of Vertex Cover using k O(k ) n3
arithmetic operations. The topic of counting yes-instances of parameterized problems
has been largely unexplored until now, so there are many open problems. Here is a list
of some open problems that come to mind. In this list, all graphs are labeled unless
specified otherwise.

   • Is there an algorithm for counting yes-instances of Vertex Cover that runs in
               2                                  2
      time 2O(k ) nO(1) ? How about in time 2o(n ) ?

                                            222
Concluding Remarks and Open Problems                                             Chapter 5


   • Is there an FPT algorithm for counting yes-instances of Cluster Vertex Dele-
     tion, Feedback Vertex Set in Tournaments, or Split Vertex Deletion
     whose running time is single exponential in k?

   • Is there an FPT sampling algorithm that generates random yes-instances of Clus-
     ter Vertex Deletion, Feedback Vertex Set in Tournaments, or Split
     Vertex Deletion?

   • Are there FPT algorithms for counting yes-instances of other vertex deletion prob-
     lems, such as Cograph Vertex Deletion or Feedback Vertex Set in gen-
     eral graphs?

   • So far, all known algorithms for counting yes-instances of a vertex deletion problem
     are inspired by the FPT algorithm or kernel for the corresponding deletion problem.
     Is there an FPT (or perhaps even polynomial-time) algorithm for counting yes-
     instances of a W[1]-hard vertex deletion problem (such as deletion to bounded
     degeneracy graphs, perfect graphs, or weakly chordal graphs)? Or can this be
     ruled out under plausible assumptions?

   To design the faster algorithm (with single-exponential running time) for counting
yes-instances of Vertex Cover in Chapter 4, we used ideas inspired by the Buss kernel
for Vertex Cover. It is likely that one could also design a faster algorithm for counting
yes-instances of Cluster Vertex Deletion by using ideas from the kernel for that
problem.
   For graph classes where we do not yet have an FPT counting algorithm, if the graph
class can be characterized by a finite set of forbidden induced subgraphs, then one can use
the inclusion-exclusion approach from Chapter 4 to reduce to a variation of the problem
with given deletion sets. As long as we can design an FPT algorithm for the problem with

                                           223
given deletion sets, then we will also have an FPT algorithm for the original counting
problem.
